Some aspects of descent theory and applications
Rui Rodrigues de Abreu Fernandes Prezado

Abstract
This thesis is an exposition of the author’s contribution on effective descent morphisms in various categories of generalized categorical structures. It consists of: Chapter 1, where an elementary description of descent theory and the content of each remaining chapter is provided, supplemented with references; Chapter 2, consisting of various descent theoretical definitions and results employed in the remainder of this work; four chapters, each corresponding to an article written by the author during the period of his PhD studies. In Chapter 3, we describe conditions for which a 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functor is an effective descent morphism in the category 𝒱 ​ - ​ 𝖢𝖺𝗍 \mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat} of 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -categories, where 𝒱 \mathcal{V} is a cartesian monoidal category with finite limits. Since these conditions rely on understanding (effective) descent morphisms in the free coproduct completion 𝖥𝖺𝗆 ⁡ ( 𝒱 ) \mathsf{Fam}(\mathcal{V}) of the category 𝒱 \mathcal{V} , we also carried out a study of such morphisms. We show how these results may be applied to describe the effective descent 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functors for the categories 𝒱 = 𝖢𝖧𝖺𝗎𝗌 \mathcal{V}=\mathsf{CHaus} of compact Hausdorff spaces and 𝒱 = 𝖲𝗍𝗇 \mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Stn} of Stone spaces. The main reference of this chapter is the single-authored article On effective descent 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functors and familial descent morphisms , published in the Journal of Pure and Applied Algebra, vol. 228, n. 5, 2024 . We study effective descent morphisms for generalized multicategories internal to a category 𝒱 \mathcal{V} with finite limits in Chapter 4, proposing two approaches to obtain their description. The first approach relies on depicting the category 𝖢𝖺𝗍 ⁡ ( T , 𝒱 ) \mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V}) of T T -categories internal to 𝒱 \mathcal{V} as a 2-dimensional limit, which provides a method of studying their effective descent morphisms. The second approach extends Ivan Le Creurer’s techniques on internal categories to the setting of generalized internal multicategories. As a consequence of this work, we provide conditions for functors between internal multicategories to be of effective descent, as well as for functors between internal graded categories (by an internal monoid), internal operadic multicategories and “enhanced” multicategories. The main reference for this chapter is the article Descent for internal multicategory functors , published in Applied Categorical Structures, vol. 31, n. 11, 2023 , with Fernando Lucatelli Nunes. Furnished with the results for effective descent morphisms in internal generalized multicategories, Chapter 5 aims to extend these results to the setting of enriched generalized multicategories – the so-called ( T , 𝒱 ) (T,\mathcal{V}) -categories. This is accomplished by extending the embedding of “enriched → \to internal” categories to the setting of generalized multicategories, via a broad notion of change-of-base for generalized categorical structures, which we specialize to our setting. We discuss the conditions under which the embedding ( T ¯ , 𝒱 ) ​ - ​ 𝖢𝖺𝗍 → 𝖢𝖺𝗍 ⁡ ( T , 𝒱 ) (\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V}) exists and whether it reflects effective descent morphisms. Finally, we show these results can be applied to the enriched counterparts of the multicategories considered in Chapter 4. More precisely, we obtain descriptions of the effective descent functors between enriched multicategories, enriched graded categories, enriched operadic multicategories, and the discrete counterparts to the “enhanced” multicategories. The main reference for this chapter is the article Generalized multicategories: change-of-base, embedding and descent, arXiv:2309.08084, DMUC preprints 23-29 , under review, with Fernando Lucatelli Nunes. Chapter 6 considers the techniques used by Sobral to study effective descent functors with respect to the fibration of discrete opfibrations under a new perspective. More specifically, we first highlight the relationship between the Cauchy completion of 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -enriched categories and the 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -fully faithful lax epimorphisms: the latter are precisely those 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functors that induce an equivalence on the Cauchy completions. Second, we show that the study of effective descent functors with respect to a suitable pseudofunctor 𝖢𝖺𝗍 𝗈𝗉 → 𝖢𝖠𝖳 \mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT} can be simplified via formal methods. Combining these two ideas, we confirm that Sobral’s characterization can be extended, showing the same conditions also characterize the effective descent morphisms with respect to the fibration of split opfibrations . The main reference for this chapter is the article Cauchy completeness, lax epimorphisms and effective descent for split fibrations , published in Bulletin of the Belgian Mathematical Society – Simon Stevin, vol. 30, n. 1, 2023 , with Fernando Lucatelli Nunes and Lurdes Sousa. \setsinglecolumn Resumo Esta tese é uma exposição das contribuições do autor sobre morfismos de descida efetiva em várias categorias de estruturas categoriais generalizadas. É consistido por: Capítulo 1, onde é fornecida uma descrição elementar sobre a teoria de descida e o conteúdo dos demais capítulos, complementado com referências bibliográficas; Capítulo 2, composto por várias definições e vários resultados da teoria de descida empregues na restante obra; quatro Capítulos, correspondendo a cada artigo escrito pelo autor durante o período dos seus estudos doutorais. No Capítulo 3, descrevemos condições para que um 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functor seja de um morfismo de descida efetiva na categoria 𝒱 ​ - ​ 𝖢𝖺𝗍 \mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat} de 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -categorias, onde 𝒱 \mathcal{V} é uma categoria monoidal cartesiana com limites finitos. Como estas condições dependem do conhecimento dos morfismos de descida (efetiva) no completamento livre 𝖥𝖺𝗆 ⁡ ( 𝒱 ) \mathsf{Fam}(\mathcal{V}) da categoria 𝒱 \mathcal{V} para coprodutos, também se desempenhou um estudo de tais morfismos. Demostramos como estes resultados podem ser aplicados para descrever os 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functores de descida efetiva para as categorias 𝒱 = 𝖢𝖧𝖺𝗎𝗌 \mathcal{V}=\mathsf{CHaus} de espaços de Hausdorff compactos e 𝒱 = 𝖲𝗍𝗇 \mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Stn} de espaços de Stone. A referência principal deste capítulo é o artigo de autoria única On effective descent 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functors and familial descent morphisms , publicado no Journal of Pure and Applied Algebra, vol. 228, n. 5, 2024 . Estudamos morfismos de descida efetiva para multicategorias generalizadas internas a uma categoria 𝒱 \mathcal{V} com limites finitos no Capítulo 4, propondo duas abordagens para obter a sua descrição. A primeira abordagem recorre a uma descrição da categoria 𝖢𝖺𝗍 ⁡ ( T , 𝒱 ) \mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V}) de T T -categorias internas a 𝒱 \mathcal{V} como um limite de dimensão 2, que proporciona um método para o estudo dos seus morfismos de descida efetiva. A segunda abordagem extende as técnicas de Ivan Le Creurer para a descrição de morfismos de descida efetiva em categorias internas para o contexto das multicategorias internas generalizadas. Como consequência destas descrições, fornecemos condições para que functores entre multicategorias internas sejam de descida efetiva, tal como functores entre categorias graduadas internas (por um monóide interno), multicategorias operádicas internas, e multicategorias “aprimoradas”. A referência principal para este capítulo é o artigo Descent for internal multicategory functors , publicado em Applied Categorical Structures, vol. 31, nº 11, 2023 , com Fernando Lucatelli Nunes. Munido com os resultados sobre morfismos de descida efetiva em multicategorias generalizadas internas, o Capítulo 5 pretende extender estes resultados para o contexto das multicategorias generalizadas enriquecidas – as ditas ( T , 𝒱 ) (T,\mathcal{V}) -categorias. Isto foi concretizado através de uma extensão da imersão de categorias “enriquecidas → \to internas” para o ambiente das multicategorias generalizadas, através de uma noção abrangente de mudança de base para estruturas categoriais generalizadas, que especializamos para o nosso contexto. São também discutidas as condições sob as quais é possível levar a cabo uma tal extensão, e quando é, se ( T ¯ , 𝒱 ) ​ - ​ 𝖢𝖺𝗍 → 𝖢𝖺𝗍 ⁡ ( T , 𝒱 ) (\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V}) reflete morfismos de descida efetiva. Finalmente, demostra-se que estes resultados podem ser aplicados às multicategorias enriquecidas associadas às consideradas no Capítulo 4. Mais precisamente, obtemos descrições para os morfismos de descida efetiva entre multicategorias enriquecidas, categorias graduadas enriquecidas, multicategorias operádicas enriquecidas, e os análogos discretos das multicategorias “aprimoradas”. A referência principal para este capítulo é o artigo Generalized multicategories: change-of-base, embedding and descent, arXiv:2309.08084, pré-publicações DMUC 23-29 , sob revisão, com Fernando Lucatelli Nunes. O Capítulo 6 considera as técnicas utilizadas por Sobral no estudo de morfismos de descida efetiva em relação ao fibrado dos opfibrados discretos sob uma nova perspetiva. Mais especificamente, realçamos, em primeiro lugar, a relação entre o completamento de Cauchy para categorias enriquecidas em 𝒱 \mathcal{V} e os epimorfismos lassos 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -plenamente fiéis: estes últimos são precisamente os 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -functores que induzem uma equivalência nos completamentos de Cauchy. Em segundo lugar, mostramos que o estudo de morfismos de descida efetiva em relação a um pseudofunctor 𝖢𝖺𝗍 𝗈𝗉 → 𝖢𝖠𝖳 \mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT} pode ser simplificado através de métodos formais. Combinando estas duas ideias, confirmamos que a caracterização de Sobral pode ser extendida, mostrando que as mesmas condições também caracterizam os morfismos de descida efetiva em relação ao pseudofunctor de opfibrados cindidos . A referência principal para este capítulo é o artigo Cauchy completeness, lax epimorphisms and effective descent for split fibrations , publicado em Bulletin of the Belgian Mathematical Society – Simon Stevin, vol. 30, nº 1, 2023 , com Fernando Lucatelli Nunes e Lurdes Sousa.

Chapter 1
Introduction

Descent theory
was first established in
Grothendieck, 1960
;
Giraud, 1964
;
Demazure, 1970
;
Grothendieck, 1971
in the context of algebraic geometry, aiming to generalize the solution
of the following problem: describe the commutative ring homomorphisms
R\to S
for which the extension-of-scalars functor
R\text{-}\mathsf{Mod}\to S\text{-}\mathsf{Mod}
is
well-behaved. A more recent account of this problem, studied in a broader
context, can be found in
Janelidze & Tholen, 2004
.

Descent theory has since found various applications and connections with
other areas of mathematics, namely:

–

the theory of monads
Bénabou & Roubaud, 1970
,
Lucatelli, 2021
,
Lucatelli, 2022
,

–

two-dimensional limits and coherence,
Lack, 2002
,
Lucatelli, 2016
,

Lucatelli, 2018a
,

–

algebraic topology
Brown & Janelidze, 1997
,
Clementino & Hofmann, 2012
,

–

Janelidze-Galois theory
Janelidze, 1990
,
Borceux & Janelidze, 2001
,

–

topology
Reiterman & Tholen, 1994
,
Clementino & Hofmann, 2002
.

It is often useful to depict descent theory as a higher dimensional analogue
of
sheaf theory
, as in
Janelidze & Tholen, 1994
, Introduction
. The
gluing
condition
, described in terms of an equalizer of a parallel pair of functions
on sets (
sheaf condition
), is replaced by
descent data
,
described in terms of a descent object of a suitable diagram of categories
(
descent condition
).

1.1
Descent theory with respect to the basic bifibration

The fundamental setting begins with a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks, a
morphism
p\colon e\to b
, and considers the following

change-of-base
adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\downarrow b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\downarrow e,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
\mathcal{C}\downarrow x
is the comma category whose objects are the
morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
with codomain
x
, also called
bundles

over
x
. The descent problem, in this setting, can be stated as follows:
describe the morphisms
p\colon e\to b
where bundles over
b
admit
a presentation as bundles over
e
plus some structure, specified by
p^{*}
, satisfying coherence conditions – this structure is the so-called

descent data
.

The
category

\mathsf{Desc}(p)

of descent data of
p
may
be presented as the category
T^{p}\text{-}\mathsf{Alg}
of
T^{p}
-algebras
,
where
T^{p}
is the monad induced by the change-of-base adjunction, by the
Bénabou-Roubaud theorem
Bénabou & Roubaud, 1970
. Hence, we may consider the

Eilenberg-Moore factorization
of the pullback functor
p^{*}
as
follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\downarrow b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\downarrow e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Desc}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}

Therefore, the descent problem is reduced to the question of whether the
comparison functor
\mathcal{K}^{p}\colon\mathcal{C}\downarrow b\to\mathsf{Desc}(p)
is
an equivalence. When this is the case, we say that
p
is an

effective descent morphism
: these morphisms, the main object of study
of this work, are precisely the solutions to the descent problem. Therefore it
is informative to obtain descriptions of such morphisms. In a pursuit of such
descriptions, it is useful to consider the notions of
descent
and

almost descent
morphism. We say that
p
is

–

p
is a descent morphism if
\mathcal{K}^{p}
is fully faithful,

–

p
is an almost descent morphism in
\mathcal{K}^{p}
is faithful.

These refinements will be useful in our description of effective descent
morphisms in categorical structures.

If
\mathcal{C}
has all finite limits, then

–

the descent morphisms are precisely the pullback-stable regular
epimorphisms, and

–

the almost descent morphisms are precisely the pullback-stable
epimorphisms,

but, in general
1
1

1

If
\mathcal{C}
is either Barr-exact
Barr, 1971
or
locally cartesian closed, the effective descent morphisms are precisely the
regular epimorphisms.
, the effective descent morphisms seldom have an
elementary description. In fact, our story begins with the classical example
of this phenomenon: the characterization of effective descent morphisms in the
category
\mathsf{Top}
of topological spaces, first given in
Reiterman & Tholen, 1994
, shows
how involved such a description can get.

In
Le, 1999
, we find another example of the prominently challenging
problem of studying effective descent morphisms. This work studies these
morphisms in categories of essentially algebraic theories internal to a
category
\mathcal{B}
with finite limits. In particular, given a functor
p\colon x\to y
of categories internal to
\mathcal{B}
, Le Creurer shows
that
p
is effective for descent if
2
2

2

Le Creurer required the
component of
p
on objects,
p_{0}\colon x_{0}\to y_{0}
to be effective for
descent, but this was shown to be redundant in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma A.3
.
.

(I)

p_{1}\colon x_{1}\to y_{1}
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathcal{B}
,

(II)

p_{2}\colon x_{2}\to y_{2}
is a descent morphism in
\mathcal{B}
,

(III)

p_{3}\colon x_{3}\to y_{3}
is an almost descent morphism in
\mathcal{B}
,

where
p_{n}\colon x_{n}\to y_{n}
is the component of
p
on the object of
the
n
-tuples of composable morphisms (or
n
-chains
).
Moreover, when
\mathcal{B}
is lextensive and has a (regular epi,
mono)-factorization system, it was also verified that these criteria are
necessary.

The effective descent morphisms in the category of finite ordered sets
(equivalent to the category of finite topological spaces) were studied in

Janelidze & Sobral, 2002
, proving that a morphism
p\colon x\to y
between finite
ordered sets is effective for descent if and only if for all
a,b,c\in y

with
a\leqslant b\leqslant c
there exist
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in x
with
a^{\prime}\leqslant b^{\prime}\leqslant c^{\prime}
such that
a=pa^{\prime}
,
b=pb^{\prime}
and
c=pc^{\prime}
. We point
out the similarity of this condition with
(II)
, as any
ordered set is a category. This insight of “chain-surjectivity” led

Clementino & Hofmann, 2002
to show that a suitable restatement of these conditions provided a
neat perspective on the characterization of the effective descent morphisms in

\mathsf{Top}
, which arise naturally once topological spaces are incarnated as a

generalized categorical structure
. Inspired by this perspective,

Clementino & Hofmann, 2012
;
Clementino & Hofmann, 2017
;
Clementino & Hofmann, 2004
;
Clementino & Janelidze, 2011
studied the description problem of effective
descent morphisms in various notions of
spaces
; when
\mathcal{V}
is
a suitable quantale, these are the so-called
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories
, which were introduced in a more general setting in

Clementino & Tholen, 2003
. These categories of
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories are a notion of

enriched
generalized categorical structure, of which the category
\mathsf{Top}
of topological spaces is an example. This corroborates the perspective
of Lawvere, given in
Lawvere, 1973
, that
fundamental structures
, such
as topological spaces, are
categorical
in nature.

The insight of
Le, 1999
;
Janelidze & Sobral, 2002
;
Clementino & Hofmann, 2002
supports the intuition that effective
descent morphisms of categorical structures have a natural description in
terms of the “chain-surjectivity” conditions
(I)
,

(II)
and
(III)
. As an example, via
his study on the commutativity of bilimits, Lucatelli Nunes obtained

Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.10
, where it was shown that the embedding of the
category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
of
\mathcal{V}
-categories into the category
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})

of categories internal to
\mathcal{V}

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})

(1.1)

reflects effective descent functors, when
\mathcal{V}
is a suitable
lextensive, cartesian monoidal category. Together with the
“chain-surjectivity” criteria of Le Creurer, we recover a list of criteria
for such enriched
\mathcal{V}
-functors to be effective for descent.

This is where the work of the author comes in. It was shown in
Prezado, 2024

that

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}

reflects effective descent morphisms, when
\mathcal{V}
is a category with
finite limits (
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 3.1
), where
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is the category of

families
of objects of
\mathcal{V}
, also known as the
free
coproduct completion
of
\mathcal{V}
. The category
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is a suitable
lextensive category, so that
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.10
can be applied to the
embedding
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(\Fam(\mathcal{V}))
, confirming that it reflects effective
descent morphisms. Thus, by reflecting along the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}(\Fam(\mathcal{V})),}

the “chain–surjectivity” criteria of
Le, 1999
allow us to describe the
effective descent morphisms in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
in terms of morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
(
Prezado, 2024
, Theorem 3.3
), extending
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.10
to all
categories
\mathcal{V}
with finite limits.

The conditions stated in
Prezado, 2024
, Theorem 3.3
for effective descent
morphisms in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
are stated in terms of conditions on morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
. This naturally prompts the study of effective descent morphisms in
free coproduct completions, which was also carried out in
Prezado, 2024
. Via
these results, we can show that, when
\mathcal{V}
is a frame, we obtain one
implication of the main results of
Clementino & Hofmann, 2004
(
Prezado, 2024
, Theorem 4.7
),
effectively confirming that the criterion set forth by
Janelidze & Sobral, 2002
are
implied by the criteria of
Le, 1999
, confirming that both approaches to
seemingly unrelated descent problems have the same underlying ideas.

The work of
Clementino & Hofmann, 2002
;
Clementino & Hofmann, 2012
;
Clementino & Hofmann, 2017
;
Clementino & Janelidze, 2011
regards effective descent morphisms
of
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories
, when
\mathcal{V}
is a quantale. The
conditions described therein can also be described via similar
“chain-surjectivity” conditions, evidencing that this perspective on
effective descent morphisms goes beyond plain categorical structures.

Multicategories
are the most fundamental example of a generalized
categorical structure. An illustrative example of which is the multicategory

\mathsf{Vect}
of vector spaces and
multilinear
maps, that is, functions

f\colon V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{n}\to W
from a finite list of
vector spaces
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
to a vector space
W
, which are
linear in each component:

f(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i}+\lambda w_{i},\ldots,v_{n})=f(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i},\ldots,v_{n})+\lambda f(v_{1},\ldots,w_{i},\ldots,v_{n}),

where
v_{j}
is a vector in
V_{j}
for each
j=1,\ldots,n
,
w_{i}
is a vector in
V_{i}
, and
\lambda
is a scalar. This definition
includes
n=0
; in which case
f
consists of a vector in
W
.

Thus, multicategories generalize categories in the sense that the domain of a
morphism consists of a finite string of objects, with an adequate notion of
composition of morphisms, as well as identity morphisms, satisfying suitable
associativity and unity laws. A more thorough introduction to these objects
can be found in Chapter
4
, along with references for
further study.

More general notions of “multicategory” can be obtained by varying the
“shape” of the domain of a morphism. In the case of categories, the
“shape” is just an object, while in the multicategory case, the “shape” is
a finite string of objects. As we have mentioned, topological spaces are
generalized categorical structures. In this case, for a topological space

X
, the domains of the morphisms are
ultrafilters
on its
underlying set of objects (points), and a morphism
\mathfrak{x}\to x
is
the
assertion
that the ultrafilter
\mathfrak{x}
converges to the
point
x
.
3
3

3

This is analogous to the notion that a ordered set
X

can be viewed as a category, whose morphisms
x\to y
are the assertions that
“
x
is related to
y
”.
This perspective on topological spaces can be
traced back to
Barr, 1970
.

As in the case of plain categories, we have a notion of
internal

generalized multicategories, first considered in
Burroni, 1971
, and more
recently in
Hermida, 2000
, as well as a notion of
enriched
generalized
multicategories, which are the
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories of
Clementino & Tholen, 2003
. Our
goal is to obtain an uniform description of the effective descent morphisms on
both accounts of generalized categorical structure.

Towards such a description, the work carried out in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
is our first
step, where we studied the effective descent morphisms of

T
-categories
4
4

4

Here,
T
is a suitable monad on a
\mathcal{V}

with finite limits which models the shape of the domains of the morphisms.

internal to
\mathcal{V}
. Therein, we showed that, if
\mathcal{V}
is a
category with finite limits, then any functor of
T
-categories internal to

\mathcal{V}
satisfying a suitable notion of “chain-surjectivity” conditions
is an effective descent morphism (
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Theorem 5.3
), extending the
result of
Le, 1999
to the setting of internal generalized categorical
structures. In particular, our results provide insight into the effective
descent functors of (ordinary) multicategories internal to any category with
finite limits, such as
\mathsf{Set}
or
\mathsf{Top}
.

Based on
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, and inspired by the techniques of
Lucatelli, 2018a
, the work
developed in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
considers the problem of reflecting effective
descent morphisms along a suitable embedding “enriched
\to
internal”
in the setting of generalized multicategories, analogous to
(
1.1
). Therein, a notion of
change-of-base
for
generalized multicategories was developed
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Theorem 5.2
, and it
was shown that, under suitable conditions (for instance, when
\mathcal{V}
is
lextensive), the natural generalization of (
1.1
) to the setting
of generalized multicategories

(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})

(1.2)

is an embedding (Theorem 9.2) and reflects effective descent morphisms. Thus,
by applying the results of
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, we obtain a description of effective
descent morphisms for enriched generalized multicategories in terms of
“chain-surjectivity” conditions (Theorem 10.5).

The topic of obtaining the relationship between the work of
Clementino & Hofmann, 2002
;
Clementino & Hofmann, 2012
;
Clementino & Hofmann, 2017
;
Clementino & Janelidze, 2011
and
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
regarding effective descent morphisms of enriched
generalized multicategories is still the subject of on-going work.

1.2
Descent theory with respect to a pseudofunctor

The descent problem in
Grothendieck, 1960
was stated in a more general setting.
For each object
x
in
\mathcal{C}
, we replace the category of bundles
\mathcal{C}\downarrow x
by a category
Fx
of “structures” over
x
, and
for each morphism
p\colon e\to b
, we have a change-of-base functor
p^{*}\colon Fb\to Fe
, replacing the pullback functor. Together with
coherent isomorphisms
\mathsf{id}^{*}_{x}\cong\mathsf{id}_{Fx}
and
(r\circ p)^{*}\cong p^{*}\circ r^{*}
for a morphism
r\colon b\to c
,
F
defines a

pseudofunctor

\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
.

As was the case for the basic bifibration, we can also define a category
\mathsf{Desc}_{F}(p)
of
F
-descent data for
p
for any pseudofunctor
F
,
for which we obtain a factorization of
p^{*}
– the
F
-descent
factorization
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fe}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Desc}_{F}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{p}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}

(1.3)

We say that
p
is an
effective
F
-descent
(
F
-descent)
morphism if
\mathcal{K}^{p}_{F}
is an equivalence (fully faithful).

The main objects of study of
Sobral, 2004
are the (effective) descent
morphisms with respect to the pseudofunctor

F=\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})\colon\mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}

of discrete opfibrations. Therein, Sobral has shown that any functor
p\colon e\to b
between small categories has a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(1.4)

whose image via
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})
is equivalent to the
F
-descent
factorization (
1.3
) with
F=\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})
.
Consequently, we have an equivalence
\theta\colon\mathsf{Desc}_{F}(p)\simeq\mathsf{CAT}(k_{p},\mathsf{Set})
such that
\mathcal{K}_{F}^{p}=\mathsf{CAT}(\phi,\mathsf{Set})\circ\theta
.

In this way, the relevance of the notion of
lax epimorphism
becomes
transparent. We say that a functor
f\colon c\to d
between small
categories is a
lax epimorphism

Adámek et al., 2001
if
\mathsf{CAT}(f,\mathsf{Set})

is fully faithful. Thus, it follows that
p
is a
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})
-descent morphism if and only if
\phi
is a lax epimorphism

Sobral, 2004
, Theorem 1
. Moreover
p
is an effective
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})
-descent morphism if and only if
\mathsf{CAT}(\phi,\mathsf{Set})
is an equivalence. It
was shown in
Sobral, 2004
, Theorem 2
that this is the case if and only if
\phi
is a
fully faithful
lax epimorphism.

The work developed in
Lucatelli et al., 2023
aims to give a systematic view on the
observations of
Sobral, 2004
, aiming to apply them in other contexts. More
specifically, we confirm that the characterization given in
Sobral, 2004
can
be plainly extended to further characterize the (effective) descent morphisms
with respect to the pseudofunctor
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Cat})\colon\mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}

of
split opfibrations
, see
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Theorem 3.2
.

We begin by considering a factorization as in (
1.4
) where
k_{p}
is the
lax codescent category
for the kernel pair of
p
. When
a pseudofunctor
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
preserves lax descent
categories, it follows that
\mathcal{K}^{F}_{p}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{F}\phi
, reducing the study of whether
p
is an (effective)
F
-descent
morphism to the study of
\phi
.

The relationship of (fully faithful) lax epimorphisms with copresheaf
categories influenced the study of their relationship with the
Cauchy
completion
of a category. This work was carried out in the
\mathcal{V}
-enriched context, as it is suitable for future considerations, and to do
so, we consider the notion of
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful functors, and
\mathcal{V}
-lax epimorphisms, as studied in
Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
. We have shown that the
following are equivalent, for a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon e\to b

between small
\mathcal{V}
-categories:

–

p
is a
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful lax epimorphism,

–

the functor
p^{*}\colon\Cauchy b\to\Cauchy b
on the Cauchy
completions induced by
p
is an equivalence,

–

the change-of-base functor
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(b,\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(e,\mathcal{V})
is an equivalence,

provided
\mathcal{V}
is a suitable monoidal category.

We obtain the main result of
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Theorem 3.2, by applying our
characterization of
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful lax epimorphisms when
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Set},\mathsf{Cat}
to the formal considerations pertaining to the factorization
(
1.4
).

Outline

In Chapter
2
we provide a concise introduction to descent
theory. We begin by recalling the 2-dimensional limit known as

lax descent category

Lucatelli, 2021
, and stating its 2-dimensional
universal property in Section
2.1
. Afterwards, we proceed to
establish the fundamental notion of this thesis: that of
effective
descent morphism
with respect to a pseudofunctor
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
,
in Section
2.2
, where we also give some remarks about the

Beck-Chevalley condition
. In Section
2.3
, we focus
on the
basic bifibration
, fixing several pieces of notation and
describing the fundamental descent-theoretical results present in

Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
,
Prezado, 2024
and
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
.

Chapter
3
, which covers the work done in
Prezado, 2024
, aims to
study effective descent morphisms in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
for a cartesian monoidal
category
\mathcal{V}
with finite limits. Our first goal is to establish that

Theorem 1.1
.

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
reflects effective descent morphisms.

Theorem
1.1
is obtained via a series of observations on
pseudopullbacks and the fact that the enrichment 2-functor preserves these
2-dimensional limits. Moreover, since
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is a suitable lextensive
category, we conclude, by
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.11
, that
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(\Fam(\mathcal{V}))
reflects effective descent morphisms. Thus, if
F
is a
\mathcal{V}
-functor, we can verify whether it is effective for descent in terms of
(effective, almost) descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
(Theorem

3.10
). In turn, this motivated us to study the (effective)
descent morphisms in the free coproduct completions of categories with finite
limits. We apply these results when
\mathcal{V}
is

–

a (co)complete Heyting lattice, establishing the connection between the
“chain-surjectivity” ideas from
Le, 1999
and
Janelidze & Sobral, 2002
,

–

a regular category, such as the categories
\mathsf{CHaus}
of compact
Hausdorff spaces and
\mathsf{Stn}
of Stone spaces,

This aforementioned connection between
Le, 1999
and
Janelidze & Sobral, 2002
helps
solidify our intuition and general understanding of the problem of effective
descent in categorical structures. More precisely, we recover one implication
of
Clementino & Hofmann, 2004
, Theorem 2.5
for Heyting lattices
\mathcal{V}
, which, when
taking
\mathcal{V}=2
, also recovers the “chain surjectivity” of

Janelidze & Sobral, 2002
, confirming the link with the approach of
Le, 1999
.

In Chapter
4
, covering the work done in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
,
we begin with an overview of the notion of generalized internal
multicategories, studied in
Burroni, 1971
and
Hermida, 2000
. After illustrating
the approach carried out in Section
4.2
for the simpler setting
of
reflexive
T
-graphs
, we provide a description of the category
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
of
internal
T
-categories
via a 2-dimensional limit, via
which we describe the effective descent morphisms, Theorem

4.10
. This is one of the approaches; we give a second approach
to the study of effective descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
via direct
calculation, closely following the ideas of
Le, 1999
, Chapter 3
, Theorem

4.13
. We finish the chapter by applying our results to
various sorts of generalized multicategory, among them are the graded,
operadic and enhanced multicategories.

In Chapter
5
, covering the relevant definitions and
the descent theoretical results of
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, we revisit (a slight
generalization of) the notion of
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories defined in

Clementino & Tholen, 2003
, under the terminology
enriched
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories
.
Afterwards, we give a few concise remarks regarding the change-of-base functor
“enriched
\to
internal” in the context of generalized multicategories,
mentioning only the most fundamental definitions to fix the notation, and
ideas for the results. Having established the embedding under suitable
conditions, we then study the problem of reflection of effective descent
morphisms, and, via Theorem
4.13
, we obtain our main result,
Theorem
5.6
, providing a description of the effective descent
morphisms in the enriched generalized multicategory setting. The chapter ends
with some brief comments on the scope of our results, and we list some
examples.

In Chapter
6
, covering the work of
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, the goal
is to study the effective descent morphisms with respect to the bifibration of

split opfibrations
. The main observation is that the results of

Sobral, 2004
on effective descent morphisms for the bifibration of

discrete opfibrations
carry over exactly to our setting (Theorem

6.11
). Indeed, we verify that a functor is of effective
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Cat})
-descent if and only if it is of effective
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})
-descent. These results are obtained via a study of the relationship between
the Cauchy completion of a category and the fully faithful, lax epimorphisms;
this study was carried out in the enriched setting, alluding to future work.

-functors and familial descent

R. Prezado, F. Lucatelli Nunes. Generalized multicategories:

DMUC preprints
23–29,
under review.

Chapter 2
A primer on descent theory

This chapter aims to give a concise introduction to classical descent theory,
under a categorical point of view, as well as to uniformize the notation and
gather the preliminary results from
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
,
Lucatelli et al., 2023
,
Prezado, 2024

and
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
pertaining to descent theory.

We begin by reviewing the 2-dimensional limit known as
lax descent
category

Street, 1976
, p. 177
,
Lucatelli, 2018
;
Lucatelli, 2021
;
Lucatelli, 2022
in Section

2.1
, which is the fundamental notion encompassing the idea of
coherence, and we state its universal property. Part of the work developed in
Chapter
3
is done directly under the perspective of the lax
descent category, particularly in Section
3.3
.

The fundamental notion to our work, that of
effective descent
morphism
with respect to a pseudofunctor
F\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
,
is presented in Section
2.2
. We also provide a few remarks on
the
Beck-Chevalley
condition (introduced in
Bénabou & Roubaud, 1970
), and its
importance in the relationship between monadicity and descent theory, as
evidenced by the
Bénabou-Roubaud theorem
.

In Section
2.3
, we begin our study of descent theory with
respect to the
basic bifibration

\mathcal{C}\downarrow-\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}

associated to a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks. We establish the
preliminary descent theoretical tools which are employed in Chapters

4
and
5
, as well as most of
Chapter
3
, namely, classical results regarding the

reflection
of effective descent morphisms along an embedding
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
(Propositions
2.4
and Corollary

2.5
), descent-theoretical results via bilimits
(Propositions
2.6
and
2.7
), as well as
an original result,
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 2.5
, regarding preservation of descent
morphisms (Lemma
2.8
). We also recall results from

Le, 1999
and
Lucatelli, 2018a
regarding effective descent morphisms of
categorical structures as our starting point (Theorems
2.10
and

2.12
).

We finish this preliminary chapter with Section
2.4
,
where we give some remarks about the descent theory with respect to the

bifibration of split opfibrations

\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Cat})\colon\mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
. Chapter
6
studies descent theory with
respect to this pseudofunctor, which is an important example of a bifibration
that does not satisfy the Beck-Chevalley condition.

2.1
Lax descent category

Throughout this work, we let
\mathsf{CAT}
be the 2-category of (large)
categories, functors and natural transformations, and we let
i\colon\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{CAT}
be the full sub-2-category of small categories.

The present definition of lax descent category follows the approach of

Lucatelli, 2021
, Section 1
of considering the 2-dimensional limit of a
pseudofunctor
\Delta_{3}\to\mathsf{CAT}
, as opposed to the approach via a
2-functor
\Delta_{\mathsf{str}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
carried out in
Lucatelli, 2022
, where
\Delta_{\mathsf{str}}
is a strict replacement of
\Delta_{3}
.

To fix the notation, we briefly recall the definition of the truncated
cosimplicial diagram
\Delta_{3}
and of a pseudofunctor. We define the
category
\Delta_{3}
to be generated by the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

with relations

\displaystyle s_{0}\circ d_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{id}_{1},

\displaystyle d_{2}\circ d_{1}

\displaystyle=d_{1}\circ d_{1},

\displaystyle s_{0}\circ d_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{id}_{1},

\displaystyle d_{0}\circ d_{0}

\displaystyle=d_{1}\circ d_{0},

\displaystyle d_{2}\circ d_{0}

\displaystyle=d_{0}\circ d_{1}.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. A
pseudofunctor

F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{CAT}
consists of

–

a function
\ob F\colon\ob\mathcal{C}\to\ob\mathsf{CAT}
,

–

a function
F_{x,y}\colon\mathcal{C}(x,y)\to\mathsf{CAT}(Fx,Fy)
, for each
pair of objects
x,y
in
\mathcal{C}
,

–

a natural isomorphism
\unit^{F}_{x}\colon\mathsf{id}_{Fx}\to F(\mathsf{id}_{x})
for each
object
x
in
\mathcal{C}
,

–

a natural isomorphism
\mult^{F}_{f,g}\colon Fg\circ Ff\to F(g\circ f)
for each pair of morphisms
f\colon x\to y
,
g\colon y\to z
in
\mathcal{C}
,

such that the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\mathsf{id}_{y})\circ Ff}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{F}_{y}\cdot Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}_{f,\mathsf{id}_{y}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff\circ F(\mathsf{id}_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff\cdot\unit^{F}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}_{\mathsf{id}_{x},f}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fh\circ Fg\circ Ff}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(h\circ g)\circ Ff}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fh\circ F(g\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(h\circ g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}_{g,h}\cdot Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fh\cdot\mult^{F}_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}_{f,h\circ g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}_{g\circ f,h}}

for all morphisms
f\colon x\to y
,
g\colon y\to z
and
h\colon z\to w
.

For a pseudofunctor
F\colon\Delta_{3}\to\mathsf{CAT}
, we write the underlying
diagram as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F3}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}}

and we define the following natural isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\upsilon^{F}_{1}

\displaystyle={\mult^{F}_{d_{1},s_{0}}}^{-1}\circ\unit^{F}_{1}\colon\mathsf{id}\to s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1},

\displaystyle\theta^{F}_{01}

\displaystyle={\mult^{F}_{d_{1},d_{1}}}^{-1}\circ\mult^{F}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\colon d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{1}\to d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{1},

\displaystyle\upsilon^{F}_{0}

\displaystyle={\mult^{F}_{d_{0},s_{0}}}^{-1}\circ\unit^{F}_{1}\colon\mathsf{id}\to s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0},

\displaystyle\theta^{F}_{02}

\displaystyle={\mult^{F}_{d_{0},d_{2}}}^{-1}\circ\mult^{F}_{d_{1},d_{0}}\colon d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{0}\to d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1},

\displaystyle\theta^{F}_{12}

\displaystyle={\mult^{F}_{d_{0},d_{1}}}^{-1}\circ\mult^{F}_{d_{0},d_{0}}\colon d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{0}\to d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0}.

The
lax descent category
of a pseudofunctor
F\colon\Delta_{3}\to\mathsf{CAT}
is a category
\mathsf{Desc}(F)
whose objects, called
lax

F
-descent data
, are pairs
(x,\phi)
where
x
is an object in
F1
and
\phi\colon d^{F}_{1}(x)\to d^{F}_{0}(x)
is a morphism in
F2
satisfying the following
reflexivity
condition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{F}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{F}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}(\phi)}

(2.1)

and
transitivity
condition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{1}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{0}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{1}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{0}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1}(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{02}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}(\phi)}

(2.2)

Given lax
F
-descent data
(x,\phi)
,
(y,\psi)
, a morphism
\omega\colon(x,\phi)\to(y,\psi)
in
\mathsf{Desc}(F)
of lax
F
-descent
data consists of a morphism
\omega\colon x\to y
such that the
following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{F}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{F}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{F}(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{F}(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{F}(\omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{F}(\omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

2.1.1
Universal property

Associated to the lax descent category, we have a forgetful functor
X\colon\mathsf{Desc}(F)\to F1
given on objects by
(x,\phi)\mapsto x
,
and a natural transformation
\Phi\colon d_{1}^{F}X\to d_{0}^{F}X
given by
\Phi_{(x,\phi)}=\phi
. This pair
(X,\Phi)
makes the following
diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{F}_{1}\cdot X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{F}_{0}\cdot X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}\cdot\Phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{0}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{0}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}\cdot\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{12}\cdot X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{01}\cdot X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}\cdot\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{02}\cdot X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}\cdot\Phi}

which is just a restatement of the conditions (
2.1
) and
(
2.2
).

If
(Y,\Psi)
is a pair where
Y\colon\mathcal{A}\to F1
is a functor
and
\Psi\colon d_{1}^{F}Y\to d_{0}^{F}Y
is a natural transformation
satisfying

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{F}_{1}\cdot Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{F}_{0}\cdot Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{F}_{0}\cdot Y}

(2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}d^{F}_{0}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{0}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}d^{F}_{0}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}d^{F}_{1}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{1}\cdot\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{12}\cdot Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{01}\cdot Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{2}\cdot\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{F}_{02}\cdot Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{F}_{0}\cdot\Psi}

(2.4)

then there is a unique
G\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Desc}(F)
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Desc}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

(2.5)

commutes, and
\Psi=\Phi\cdot G
.

Let
(Z,\Xi)
be another pair where
Z\colon\mathcal{A}\to F1
is a
functor and
\Xi\colon d_{1}^{F}Z\to d_{0}^{F}Z
is a natural transformation
satisfying (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), and let

H\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Desc}(F)
be the unique functor such that
Z=XH
and
\Xi=\Phi\cdot H
. For any natural transformation
\Gamma\colon Y\to Z
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{F}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{F}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{F}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{F}Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{F}\cdot\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{F}\cdot\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}

commutes, there is a unique
\hat{\Gamma}\colon G\to H
such that
X\cdot\hat{\Gamma}=\Gamma
.

2.2
Effective descent morphisms

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with pullbacks, and
p\colon e\to b
be a
morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. The
kernel pair
of
p
, given by the
pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times_{b}p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

(2.6)

induces an
equivalence relation
internal to
\mathcal{C}
, given by
the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times_{b}p\times_{b}p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times_{b}p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

(2.7)

which we denote by
\Ker(p)\colon\Delta_{3}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
.

For any pseudofunctor
F\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
, we let
f^{*}=Ff
for every morphism
f
in
\mathcal{C}
, and
\overline{F}_{p}=F\circ\Ker(p)^{\mathsf{op}}
. We write

\mathsf{Desc}_{F}(p)=\mathsf{Desc}(\overline{F}_{p})

(2.8)

for the category of lax descent data of
\overline{F}_{p}
. Moreover, we observe that
the kernel pair (
2.6
) induces a pair
(p^{*}\colon Fb\to Fe,\,\,\Omega\colon d^{*}_{1}p^{*}\to d^{*}_{0}p^{*})
satisfying
(
2.3
) and (
2.4
), where
\Omega={\mult^{F}_{p,d_{0}}}^{-1}\circ\mult^{F}_{p,d_{1}}
. Thus, by the universal property
(
2.5
), we obtain a unique functor
\mathcal{K}^{p}_{F}\colon Fb\to\mathsf{Desc}_{F}(p)
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fe}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Desc}_{F}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{p}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}^{p}_{F}}

(2.9)

and
\Omega=\Phi\cdot p^{*}
, where
(\mathcal{U}^{p}_{F},\Phi)
is the pair
associated to the lax descent category of
\overline{F}_{p}
. We call
(
2.9
) the
F
-descent factorization
of
p
.

We now reach the most important definition in this work. A morphism
p
in
a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks is said to be

–

an
almost
F
-descent morphism
if
\mathcal{K}^{p}_{F}
is faithful,

–

an
F
-descent morphism
if
\mathcal{K}^{p}_{F}
is fully faithful,

–

an
effective
F
-descent morphism
if
\mathcal{K}^{p}_{F}
is an
equivalence.

2.2.1
Beck-Chevalley condition

The
Beck-Chevalley condition
, introduced in
Bénabou & Roubaud, 1970
, underlies
the relationship between monadicity and descent theory. We will give a brief
remark on this topic; we recall that a pseudofunctor
F\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
is said to be a
bifibration
if, for every morphism
f
in
\mathcal{C}
, the functor
f^{*}
has a left adjoint, which we denote
by
f_{!}\dashv f^{*}
, and whose unit and counit are denoted by
\eta^{f}

and
\varepsilon^{f}
.

In this context, a bifibration is said to satisfy the
Beck-Chevalley
condition
if, for all pullback squares in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

the following natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}k^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}k^{*}g^{*}g_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}h^{*}f^{*}g_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}k^{*}\eta^{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}\theta g_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{h}f^{*}g_{!}}

is a natural isomorphism, where
\theta\colon k^{*}g^{*}\to h^{*}f^{*}
is the
induced isomorphism by the commutative square (
2.2.1
).

Theorem 2.1

(Bénabou-Roubaud
Bénabou & Roubaud, 1970
)
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
be a bifibration satisfying the
Beck-Chevalley condition. For a morphism
p\colon e\to b
in
\mathcal{C}
, we write
T^{p}
for the monad induced by the adjunction
p_{!}\dashv p^{*}
.

The
F
-descent factorization (
2.9
) of
p
is equivalent
to the Eilenberg-Moore factorization of
p^{*}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fe}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{p}\text{-}\mathsf{Alg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}^{p}}

(2.10)

so, in particular, we have an equivalence of categories

\mathsf{Desc}_{F}(p)\simeq T^{p}\text{-}\mathsf{Alg},

and the following are equivalent:

(i)

p
is an effective
F
-descent morphism (resp.
F
-descent
morphism).

(ii)

p^{*}
is monadic (resp. premonadic).

This result was generalized in
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 7.4
, via the study of
commutativity of bilimits, and the main result of
Lucatelli, 2021
(Theorem 4.7)
confirms that
(i)

\implies

(ii)
,
for
all
bifibrations (that is, not necessarily satisfying the
Beck-Chevalley condition).

2.3
Basic bifibration

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with pullbacks. For each morphism
f\colon x\to y
, we have a functor
f_{!}\colon\mathcal{C}\downarrow x\to\mathcal{C}\downarrow y
given on objects by
g\mapsto f\circ g
. For each morphism
h\colon z\to y
, we consider the following pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{f}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(h)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We observe that
\varepsilon^{f}_{h}\colon f\circ f^{*}(h)\to h
is a morphism
in
\mathcal{C}\downarrow y
, whose universal property borne out of the pullback
diagram guarantees that the assignment
f^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}\downarrow y\to\mathcal{C}\downarrow x
defines a functor right adjoint to
f_{!}
.

Together with the canonical isomorphisms
\mathsf{id}_{x}^{*}\cong\mathsf{id}_{\mathcal{C}\downarrow x}
and
f^{*}g^{*}\cong(g\circ f)^{*}
, we obtain the
basic
bifibration

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\downarrow-\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{CAT}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{C}\downarrow x

\displaystyle f\colon x\to y

\displaystyle\mapsto f^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}\downarrow y\to\mathcal{C}\downarrow x

which provides the context for our study of descent theory in Chapters

3
,
4
, and
5
.
In this setting, we write
\mathsf{Desc}(p)
instead of
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{C}\downarrow-}(p)
for the category of lax descent data, and we say “(effective/almost)
descent morphism” instead of “(effective/almost)
(\mathcal{C}\downarrow-)
-descent morphism”.

When
\mathcal{C}
is a category with finite limits, it can be shown (see

Janelidze & Tholen, 1994
, 2.4
,
Le, 1999
, Corollary 0.3.5
,
Janelidze et al., 2004
, Theorem 3.4
,

Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Proposition 2.1
) via Beck’s monadicity theorem that

–

p
is an almost descent morphism
\iff

p
is a
pullback-stable epimorphism,

–

p
is a descent morphism
\iff

p
is a pullback-stable
regular epimorphism.

While it is true that descent morphisms are effective for descent when
\mathcal{C}
is Barr-exact
Barr, 1971
or locally cartesian closed, in general,
effective descent morphisms are challenging to describe. For instance, we note
the characterization of
Reiterman & Tholen, 1994
for
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Top}
, or the
characterization of
Le, 1999
for
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
, for suitable
categories
\mathcal{V}
.

Nevertheless, the study of effective descent morphisms can be approached
directly, by studying the (essential) image of (fully faithful) comparison
functors
\mathcal{K}^{p}
for descent morphisms
p
. Thus, the following
elementary observation regarding the (essential) image of
\mathcal{K}^{p}

is of particular interest for our work.

Proposition 2.2

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Corollary 2.3
)
.

The comparison functor
\mathcal{K}^{p}
is essentially surjective if and
only if, for all descent data
(a,\gamma)
, there exists a morphism
f\colon w\to b
such that
p^{*}f\cong a
in
\mathcal{C}\downarrow x
, and

\varepsilon^{p}_{f}\circ\gamma=\varepsilon^{p}_{f}\circ\varepsilon^{p}_{p\circ a}.

(2.11)

Proof.

We begin by noting that
\mathcal{K}^{p}f=(p^{*}f,p^{*}_{\varepsilon^{p}_{f}})
is
a lax descent datum satisfying (
2.11
), by naturality.

Conversely, if
(p^{*}f,\gamma)
satisfies (
2.11
), then

\gamma=p^{*}\varepsilon^{p}_{f}\circ\eta^{p}_{p^{*}f}\circ\gamma=p^{*}\varepsilon^{p}_{f}\circ p^{*}\gamma\circ\eta^{p}_{p^{*}(p\circ p^{*}f)}=p^{*}\varepsilon^{p}_{f}\circ p^{*}\varepsilon^{p}_{p\circ a}\circ\eta^{p}_{p^{*}(p\circ p^{*}f)}=p^{*}\varepsilon^{p}_{f},

hence
(p^{*}f,\gamma)=\mathcal{K}^{p}f
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

We should point out that Proposition
2.2
is often
implicitly used in the study of effective descent morphisms (for instance,

Le, 1999
, Proposition 3.2.4
). Moreover, it can be shown that this result
also holds in the general context for a pseudofunctor
F\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
, so its applicability in descent arguments does not rely on the
Beck-Chevalley condition.

Another fruitful strategy, undertaken by both
Reiterman & Tholen, 1994
and
Le, 1999
,
and justified by the following Proposition
2.4
, is to
suitably embed the category whose effective descent morphisms we wish to study
in a larger one in which those are well-understood.

Proposition 2.4

(
Janelidze & Tholen, 1994
, 2.7
)
.

Let
U\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a fully faithful,
pullback-preserving functor between categories with pullbacks, and let
p\colon x\to y
be a morphism such that
Up
is an effective descent
morphism. Then
p
is an effective descent morphism if and only if, for
all pullback diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Uv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ux}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Uy}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Up}

(2.12)

we have
w\cong Uz
for some object
z
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Throughout our study of effective descent morphisms in categorical structures,
we have found the following particular instance of Proposition

2.4
to be particularly useful:

Corollary 2.5

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Corollary 2.5
)
.

Let
U\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a fully faithful,
pullback-preserving functor between categories with pullbacks. If for every
effective descent morphism
g\colon Ux\to z
there exists an
isomorphism
z\cong Uy
, then
U
reflects effective descent
morphisms.

Proof.

We recall that effective descent morphisms are stable under pullback. Thus,
if (
2.12
) is a pullback square, and
Up
is an
effective descent morphism, then so is
Uv\to w
by pullback-stability.
By hypothesis, there exists an isomorphism
w\cong Uy
, whence we
conclude that
p
is effective for descent by Proposition

2.4
.
∎

As one of the byproducts of the study of commutativity of bilimits carried out
in
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Lucatelli Nunes provides a description of the effective
descent morphisms for the bilimit of a diagram of categories with pullbacks and
pullback-preserving functors, in terms of the (effective) descent morphisms of
the categories in the underlying diagram. A particularly important consequence
is that this provides a second, widely applicable approach to the study of
effective descent morphisms, as exhibited by the following Propositions:

Proposition 2.6

(
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 1.6, Corollary 9.6
)
.

If we have a pseudopullback diagram of categories with pullbacks and
pullback-preserving functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}

and a morphism
f
in
\mathcal{A}
such that

–

Ff
and
Gf
are effective descent morphisms, and

–

KGf\cong HFf
is a descent morphism,

then
f
is an effective descent morphism.

Proposition 2.7

(
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.2
)
.

If we have a pseudoequalizer diagram of categories with pullback and
pullback-preserving functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

and a morphism
f
in
\mathcal{A}
such that

–

Ff
is an effective descent morphism, and

–

GFf\cong HFf
is a descent morphism,

then
f
is an effective descent morphism.

To apply Proposition
2.6
, the following result is useful:

Lemma 2.8

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 2.5
)
.

Let
H\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor between categories with
finite limits. If
H
preserves coequalizers, reflects pullbacks, and
has a fully faithful left adjoint
L\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{B}
, then
H

preserves descent morphisms.

Proof.

Let
h\colon x\to y
be a descent morphism in
\mathcal{B}
, and we
consider the following pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Hh)^{*}z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hy}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Hh)^{*}\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hh}

Since the unit
\mathsf{id}\to HL
is an isomorphism, the following is a
pullback diagram as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HL(Hh)^{*}z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HLz}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hy}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HL\varepsilon_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\psi^{\sharp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hh}

where
\phi^{\sharp}\colon Lz\to y
is obtained from
\phi
via the
hom-isomorphism
\mathcal{D}(z,Hy)\cong\mathcal{B}(Lz,y)
, and, likewise,
\psi^{\sharp}\colon L(Hh)^{*}z\to x
is obtained from
\psi=(Hh)^{*}\phi
.

Since
H
reflects pullbacks, we conclude
L\varepsilon_{\phi}
is a
regular epimorphism. This property is preserved by
H
, as it preserves
coequalizers. Thus, we conclude that
Hh
is a pullback-stable regular
epimorphism, as desired.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

The applications of Lemma
2.8
we have in mind are

–

the underlying object-of-objects functor
(-)_{0}\colon\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}
,

–

the canonical fibration
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Set}
,

–

and the underlying object-of-objects functor
(-)_{0}\colon\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}
.

Each functor has fully faithful left and right adjoints, and therefore
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
2.8
. Hence, we
conclude that each functor preserves descent morphisms.

Thanks to the first functor, we can obtain the conclusion of
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem
9.11
for
\mathcal{V}
a lextensive category such that the functor
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
1
1

1

This is the left adjoint to
\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathsf{Set}
, is fully faithful, without assuming

\mathcal{V}
has a (regular epi, mono)-factorization system, using precisely
the same proof.

The second functor plays an important role in Chapter
3
in
obtaining effective descent morphisms in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
, in the more general
setting of a cartesian monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
with finite
limits; this is Theorem
3.10
.

The third functor plays a similar role in Chapter
5
,
to obtain effective descent morphisms in a suitable category of generalized
enriched multicategories. The statement of this result is given by Theorem

5.6
.

2.3.1
Descent theory in categorical structures

In the context of effective descent morphisms in categorical structures, the
results of Le Creurer
Le, 1999
are the cornerstone upon which we obtain
our own. Indeed, since enriched categorical structures can be embedded into
an internal setting, under suitable conditions, the general strategy is to
first study the effective descent morphisms for internal categorical
structures directly (such results are given by Theorems
2.10
and

4.13
), and then apply Propositions

2.4
and
2.6
to study whether the
embedding reflects the effective descent morphisms back to the enriched
setting.

Theorem 2.10

(
Le, 1999
, Corollary 3.3.1
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a category with finite limits, and
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor of categories internal to
\mathcal{V}
. If

–

p_{0}\colon\mathcal{C}_{0}\to\mathcal{D}_{0}
is an effective descent morphism,

–

p_{1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{1}
is an effective descent morphism,

–

p_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}_{2}\to\mathcal{D}_{2}
is a descent morphism, and

–

p_{3}\colon\mathcal{C}_{3}\to\mathcal{D}_{3}
is an almost descent morphism,

then
p
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
.

Remark 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category internal to
\mathcal{V}
. Since Theorem

2.10
is stated in terms of various epimorphic conditions on the
objects on composable tuples of morphisms, it is convenient for
\mathcal{C}_{n}
to denote the object of
n
-chains
of
\mathcal{C}
, therefore
allowing “
n
-chain” to be synonymous with “composable
n
-tuple of
morphisms”.

In this vein, let
\mathcal{W}
be a monoidal category, and let
\mathcal{D}

be an enriched
\mathcal{W}
-category. For objects
x_{i}
on
\mathcal{D}

for
i=0,\,1,\,2,\,3
, we let

\mathcal{D}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})=\mathcal{D}(x_{1},x_{2})\otimes\mathcal{D}(x_{0},x_{1}),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{D}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\mathcal{D}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\otimes\mathcal{D}(x_{0},x_{1}).

Likewise, if
F\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{X}
is a lax monoidal functor, we
write

\mult^{F}\colon(F_{!}\mathcal{D})(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\to F(\mathcal{D}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}))

for the “shortened” version of the comparison morphism for the tensor
product.

As an example of a reflection result, we have the following consequence of

Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.11
and Theorem
2.10
(see also Remark

2.9
):

Theorem 2.12

(
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.11
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a lextensive category, and assume that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
is fully faithful. An enriched
\mathcal{V}
-functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
such that

–

F_{0}
is surjective,

–

F_{1}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\sum_{x_{i}}\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1})\to\sum_{y_{i}}\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1})
is an effective descent morphism,

–

F_{2}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\sum_{x_{i}}\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\to\sum_{y_{i}}\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})
is a descent morphism,

–

F_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\sum_{x_{i}}\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\to\sum_{y_{i}}\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
is an almost descent morphism,

is an effective descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
.

2.4
Bifibration of split opfibrations

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a 2-category with 2-pullbacks and lax codescent
objects
2
2

2

This is the notion dual to
lax descent object
, which
can be described in any 2-category, via the universal property in Subsection
2.1.1.
. Just like in Section
2.3
, we consider the kernel
pair
\Ker(p)
(
2.7
) of a morphism
p\colon e\to b
in
\mathbb{A}
. Since
\mathbb{A}
has lax codescent objects, there is a unique

K^{\Ker(p)}\colon\mathsf{CoDesc}(\Ker(p))\to b
making the triangle in Diagram
(
2.13
) commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times_{b}p\times_{b}p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times_{b}p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CoDesc}(\Ker(p))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\Ker(p)}}

(2.13)

Lemma 2.13

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Lemma 1.1
)
.

If a 2-functor
F\colon\mathbb{A}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
preserves lax descent
objects, then a morphism
p\colon e\to b
is of effective

F
-descent (
F
-descent) if and only if
F(K^{\Ker(p)})
is an
equivalence (fully faithful).

Proof.

When such a 2-functor
F
is composed with Diagram
(
2.13
), we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fe}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p\times_{b}p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p\times_{b}p\times_{b}p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\mathsf{CoDesc}(\Ker(p))),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(K^{\Ker(p)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fd_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fd_{0}}

(2.14)

and we observe that
F(\mathsf{CoDesc}(\Ker(p)))\simeq\mathsf{Desc}(F(\Ker(p)))
, from
which our result follows.
∎

Naturally, the representable 2-functors
\mathbb{A}(-,a)\colon\mathbb{A}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
for each object
a
preserve lax descent objects. The
bifibrations
F
,
F_{D}
of split, respectively discrete,
opfibrations are obtained by composing
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Cat})
, respectively
\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})
, with the inclusion
\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{CAT}
.

We remark that these bifibrations do not satisfy the Beck-Chevalley condition,
as the conclusion of the Bénabou-Roubaud theorem
Bénabou & Roubaud, 1970
does not hold
for
F
nor
F_{D}
. Indeed,
Sobral, 2004
, Remark 7
gives an
example of a functor
p
such that
F_{D}p
is monadic, but
p

is not an effective
F_{D}
-descent morphism.

We provide another example, given in
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Remark 3.3
: let
p\colon 1\to b
be a functor, where
1
is the terminal category. The functor

Fp=\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathsf{Cat})
is monadic if and only if
p
is
(essentially) surjective, but, as a consequence of Theorem

6.11
,
p
is an effective
F
-descent morphism
if and only if
p
is an equivalence.

Chapter 3
Enriched
\mathcal{V}
-functors

Effective descent morphisms for the category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
of
\mathcal{V}
-categories were studied in
Clementino & Hofmann, 2004
, Section 5
when
\mathcal{V}
is a
(co)complete symmetric monoidal closed thin category, and in
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem
9.11
, when
\mathcal{V}
is a lextensive, cartesian monoidal category
such that the copower functor
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
is fully
faithful. Despite the different approaches to the problem, in both works, the
conditions for a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
F
to be an effective descent
morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
are expressed in terms of surjectivity of
F
in
chains of hom-objects.

The goal of this chapter is to prove that the same conditions remain
sufficient for a
\mathcal{V}
-functor to be effective for descent when
\mathcal{V}
is a cartesian monoidal category with finite limits, placing the results
of
Clementino & Hofmann, 2004
, Section 5
(when
\mathcal{V}
is cartesian monoidal) and

Lucatelli, 2018a
on common ground. We shall prove that if a
\mathcal{V}
-functor

F
is

–

an effective descent morphism on hom-objects,

–

a descent morphism on 2-chains of hom-objects,

–

an almost descent morphism on 3-chains of hom-objects,

in a suitable sense, then
F
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
(Theorem
3.10
).

There are two key ideas for this result: if
\mathcal{V}
is a category with
finite limits, then

–

\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is a lextensive category, and
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful (Proposition
3.1
), so that we
may apply Theorem
2.12
to obtain a description of the
effective descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
, and

–

the direct image
\eta_{!}\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
of
\eta\colon\mathcal{V}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})
reflects effective descent morphisms.

Thus, we conclude that the composite functor reflects effective descent
morphisms as well. We go over the first idea in Section
3.1
,
where we review the central properties of the free coproduct completion of a
category. The second idea is obtained via Proposition

2.6
, so, in Section
3.2
we study
the relevant pseudopullbacks and their preservation by the enrichment
2-functor
\mathcal{V}\mapsto\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
.

Via the description of the effective descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
given
by Lucatelli Nunes’s result (Theorem
2.12
), we can state the
conditions for a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
F
to be an effective descent
morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
in terms of (effective, almost) descent conditions on
the underlying morphisms of
F
on chains of hom-objects. Since these refer
to morphisms in the category
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, this motivates the study of
(effective) descent morphisms in the free coproduct completion of a category,
which is carried out in Section
3.3
.

We highlight the relationship between the results of
Clementino & Hofmann, 2004
and

Lucatelli, 2018a
in Section
3.4
, where we apply our results on
(effective) descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
to study effective descent
morphisms in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
for special families of categories
\mathcal{V}
.
Among such categories, we draw our attention to the category
\mathsf{CHaus}
of
compact Hausdorff spaces and the category
\mathsf{Stn}
of Stone spaces, giving a
description of effective descent
\mathsf{CHaus}
-functors and effective descent

\mathsf{Stn}
-functors.

3.1
Properties of the free coproduct completion

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a category. The
free coproduct cocompletion
of
\mathcal{V}
, denoted
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, consists of

–

objects which given by set-indexed families
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
of objects
X_{j}
in
\mathcal{V}
,

–

morphisms
(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to(Y_{k})_{k\in K}
which are given by a
function
f\colon J\to K
, and a set-indexed family of morphisms
(\phi_{j}\colon X_{j}\to Y_{fj})_{j\in J}
, with
\phi_{j}
in
\mathcal{V}
,

with suitable identities and composition law. It may also be obtained via the
Grothendieck construction
Grothendieck, 1971
of the pseudofunctor
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
given by
X\mapsto\mathcal{V}^{X}
on objects and
f\mapsto f^{*}

on morphisms, whose fibration we denote by
P\colon\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Set}
.

We recall the following properties:

Proposition 3.1

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 3.2
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a category.

(a)

\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is extensive.

(b)

If
\mathcal{V}
has a terminal object,
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful.

(c)

If
\mathcal{V}
has finite limits, so does
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
.

Proof.

Property
(a)
is well-known, and is present in

Carboni et al., 1993
, Proposition 2.4
, for instance. Moreover, it was verified that

(b)
holds in
Borceux & Janelidze, 2001
, Proposition 6.2.1
. Property

(c)
is also well-known; see
Gray, 1966
;
Hermida, 1999
;
Borceux & Janelidze, 2001
.
Nevertheless, to illustrate the mechanics of
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, we will revisit
the arguments. Recall that

–

\mathsf{Set}
has all (finite) limits,

–

\mathcal{V}^{X}
has all finite limits, given componentwise in
\mathcal{V}
,

–

the change-of-base functor
f^{*}\colon\mathcal{V}^{Y}\to\mathcal{V}^{X}

preserves finite limits, for every function
f\colon X\to Y
,

so, if
\mathcal{A}\colon\mathcal{J}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is a finite diagram, with
\mathcal{A}_{j}=(P\mathcal{A}_{j},x_{j})
, we consider the limit cone
\lambda_{j}\colon\lim(P\mathcal{A})\to P\mathcal{A}j
, and we define a diagram

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\mathcal{J}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{V}^{\lim(P\mathcal{A})}

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\mapsto\lambda_{j}^{*}(x_{j})

and since
\mathcal{V}^{\lim(P\mathcal{A})}
has finite limits,
\lim\Phi

exists. To verify that
\lim\mathcal{A}\cong(\lim P\mathcal{A},\lim\Phi)
,
given a cone
(\gamma_{j}\colon b\to P\mathcal{A}_{j},\zeta_{j}\colon w\to\gamma_{j}^{*}x_{j})
, we let
\omega\colon b\to\lim P\mathcal{A}
be the
unique function such that
\gamma_{j}=\lambda_{j}\circ\omega
, and we
observe that
\omega^{*}(\lim\Phi)\cong\lim\omega^{*}\Phi
, since
\omega^{*}
preserves limits. Then, there exists a unique
\xi\colon w\to\omega^{*}(\lim\Phi)
such that
\zeta_{j}=\mult^{\mathcal{V}^{-}}_{\lambda_{j},\omega}\circ\omega^{*}\phi_{j}\circ\xi
, where
\phi_{j}\colon\lim\Phi\to x_{j}
is the limit cone of
\Phi
, and
\mult^{\mathcal{V}^{-}}_{\lambda_{j},\omega}\colon\omega^{*}\lambda_{j}^{*}\cong(\lambda_{j}\circ\omega)^{*}
is the natural comparison isomorphism of the
pseudofunctor.

Indeed, we have
(\gamma_{j},\zeta_{j})=(\lambda_{j},\phi_{j})\circ(\omega,\xi)
, and if
(\gamma_{j},\zeta_{j})=(\lambda_{j},\phi_{j})\circ(\theta,\chi)
, then
\gamma_{j}=\lambda_{j}\circ\theta
which
confirms
\theta=\omega
, and
\zeta_{j}=\mult^{\mathcal{V}^{-}}_{\lambda_{j},\omega}\circ\omega^{*}\phi_{j}\circ\chi
, confirming
\chi=\xi
.
∎

In particular, it follows that

Corollary 3.2

(
Prezado, 2024
, p. 10
)
.

The functor
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(\Fam(\mathcal{V}))
reflects effective descent
morphisms.

Proof.

Since
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is lextensive and
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is
fully faithful by Proposition
3.1
, we may apply
Theorem
2.12
.
∎

This result was the original motivation to study the problem of whether
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
reflects effective descent morphisms as well. In this
direction, we recall from
Weber, 2007
that the inclusion
\mathcal{V}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is a 2-cartesian natural transformation:

Proposition 3.3

(
Weber, 2007
, 5.15 Proposition
)
.

For any functor
F\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}
, the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fam(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fam(\mathcal{W})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fam(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

(3.1)

is a 2-pullback.

In particular, we note there is a unique functor
!\colon\mathcal{V}\to 1
,
and
\Fam(!)\colon\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\Fam(1)\simeq\mathsf{Set}
is the fibration
associated to
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
. Thus, by
Street & Joyal, 1993
, (
3.1
) is a
pseudopullback when
\mathcal{W}\simeq 1
.

The last preliminary result we shall need is the following reformulation of

Borceux & Janelidze, 2001
, Proposition 6.1.5
:

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a category with coproducts. If
\mathcal{C}
is a full
subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
such that

(i)

for all objects
a
of
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a set
J
and
objects
c_{j}
of
\mathcal{C}
for each
j\in J
such that
a\cong\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}
,

(ii)

for all objects
x
,
d_{k}
of
\mathcal{C}
for each
k\in K
, any morphism
x\to\sum_{k\in K}d_{k}
factors uniquely via

\iota_{k}\colon d_{k}\to\sum_{k\in K}d_{k}
for some
k\in K
,

then we have an equivalence
\Fam(\mathcal{C})\simeq\mathcal{A}
.

Under the above hypotheses, we conclude that
\mathcal{A}
is an extensive
category, and that
\mathcal{C}
(essentially) consists of the

connected
objects of
\mathcal{A}
– that is, those objects
a\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}(a,-)
preserves coproducts.

Proof.

We have a coproduct functor

\sum\colon\Fam(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{A}

(3.2)

defined on objects by
(c_{j})_{j\in J}\mapsto\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}
. We
observe that (
3.2
) is essentially surjective if and only
if
(i)
holds.

On morphisms, (
3.2
) is given by the composite

\displaystyle\Fam(\mathcal{C})((c_{j})_{j\in J},(d_{k})_{k\in K})

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{j\in J}\sum_{k\in K}\mathcal{C}(c_{j},d_{k})

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{j\in J}\sum_{k\in K}\mathcal{A}(c_{j},d_{k})

\displaystyle\to\prod_{j\in J}\mathcal{A}\big(c_{j},\sum_{k\in K}d_{k}\big)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}\big(\sum_{j\in J}c_{j},\sum_{k\in K}d_{k}\big)

where
\mathcal{A}(c_{j},d_{k})\to\mathcal{A}\big(c_{j},\sum_{k\in K}d_{k}\big)
is
given by
\mathcal{A}(c_{j},\iota_{k})
. We observe that
(ii)
holds if
and only if

\sum_{k\in K}\mathcal{A}(x,d_{k})\to\mathcal{A}\big(x,\sum_{k\in K}d_{k}\big)

is an isomorphism, so we conclude that (
3.2
) is fully
faithful if and only if
(ii)
holds.
∎

One consequential application of Proposition
3.4
is that it
allows us to reduce the study of (effective, almost) descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
to the study of
covers
, that is, morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
of
the form
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a pullback-stable class of morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
that
