is closed under coproducts (as a full subcategory of
[2,\mathcal{V}]
). We
have an equivalence of categories
\mathcal{E}\simeq\Fam(\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{conn}})
,
where
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{conn}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
is the subclass of covers in
\mathcal{E}
; that is, those morphisms of the form
\phi\colon(X_{i})_{j\in J}\to Y
.

Proof.

If
(f,\psi)\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to(Y_{k})_{k\in K}
is in
\mathcal{E}
, we consider the following pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in f^{*}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{k})_{k\in K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi|_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\psi)}

for each
k\in K
. By pullback stability, we find that
\psi|_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{conn}}
for all
k\in K
, and
\psi\cong\sum_{k\in K}\psi|_{k}
.

If
\phi\colon(V_{i})_{i\in I}\to W
is in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{conn}}
, and we
have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{k})_{k\in K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k,\omega)}

(3.3)

then
f(g(i))=k
, so that
g(i)\in f^{*}k
for all
i\in I
,
and hence (
3.3
) factors uniquely as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in f^{*}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y_{k})_{k\in K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi|_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}

so we may apply Proposition
3.4
.
∎

3.2
Embedding
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}

In
Fujii & Lack, 2024
, Corollary 3.8
, the authors have shown that the enrichment
2-functor
(-)\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\colon\mathsf{Bicat}\to 2\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
preserves all
weighted, connected 2-limits, where
\mathsf{Bicat}
is the 2-category of
bicategories, pseudofunctors and icons. Denoting by
\mathsf{SymCat}
the
2-category of symmetric monoidal categories, monoidal functors and monoidal
natural transformations, we obtain the following corollary:

Proposition 3.6

(
Fujii & Lack, 2024
, Corollary 3.8
)
.

The enrichment 2-functor
\mathsf{SymCat}\to\mathsf{CAT}
preserves pseudopullbacks.

Proof.

Pseudopullbacks are
\mathsf{Cat}
-connected limits.
∎

It is known that a morphism
p\colon a\to b
in a 2-category is fully
faithful if and only if
p\downarrow p\simeq 2\pitchfork a
1
1

1

f\downarrow g
is the
comma object
of two morphisms
f,g
in a 2-category
\mathbb{A}
.
\mathcal{C}\pitchfork x
is the
power object
of an object

x
in
\mathbb{A}
by a category
\mathcal{C}
.
. However, it was shown in

Fujii & Lack, 2024
that powers and comma objects are
not

\mathsf{Cat}
-connected limits. Despite this, we can still obtain the following result:

Lemma 3.7

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 2.2
)
.

The enrichment 2-functor
\mathsf{SymCat}\to\mathsf{CAT}
preserves fully faithful
functors.

Proof.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}
be a fully faithful monoidal functor,
and let
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
be
\mathcal{V}
-categories. A
\mathcal{W}
-functor
\Psi\colon F_{!}\mathcal{C}\to F_{!}\mathcal{D}
consists of

–

A function
\ob\Psi\colon\ob\mathcal{C}\to\ob\mathcal{D}
on objects,

–

A morphism
\Psi_{x,y}\colon F\mathcal{C}(x,y)\to F\mathcal{D}(\Psi x,\Psi y)

in
\mathcal{W}
for each pair
x,y\in\ob\mathcal{C}
.

We claim that we have a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
\Phi\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
given
\ob\Phi=\ob\Psi
and
\Phi_{x,y}
is the unique
morphism
\mathcal{C}(x,y)\to\mathcal{D}(\Psi x,\Psi y)
such that
F\Phi_{x,y}=\Psi_{x,y}
for all
x,y\in\ob\mathcal{C}
, by full
faithfulness of
F
.

Recalling that
\unit^{F_{!}\mathcal{X}}=F\unit^{\mathcal{X}}\circ\unit^{F}
and
\comp^{F_{!}\mathcal{X}}=F\comp^{\mathcal{X}}\circ\mult^{F}
for any
\mathcal{V}
-category
\mathcal{X}
, we note that the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FI}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathcal{C}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathcal{D}(\Phi x,\Phi y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\unit^{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\unit^{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\Phi_{x,y}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{!}\mathcal{C})(x,y,z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{!}\mathcal{D})(\Phi x,\Phi y,\Phi z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\mathcal{C}(x,y,z))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\mathcal{D}(\Phi x,\Phi y,\Phi z))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathcal{C}(x,z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathcal{D}(\Phi x,\Phi z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\Phi)_{x,y,z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\Phi_{x,y,z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\comp^{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\comp^{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\Phi_{x,z}}

since
\Psi
is a
\mathcal{W}
-functor. Since
\unit^{F}
and
\mult^{F}

are invertible, and
F
is fully faithful, we deduce that
\Phi
must
be a
\mathcal{V}
-functor, as desired.
∎

Lemma 3.8

(
Prezado, 2024
, p. 9
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a category with finite limits. We have a pseudopullback
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(3.4)

of categories with pullbacks and pullback-preserving functors.

Proof.

We observe that (
3.4
) is the composite of the enrichment
2-functor with the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fam(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

which is a 2-pullback by
Weber, 2007
, Proposition 5.15
, and since
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Set}
is an (iso)fibration, it is in fact a pseudopullback by

Street & Joyal, 1993
. Now the result follows by Proposition
3.6
.
∎

Theorem 3.9

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 3.1
)
.

If
\mathcal{V}
has finite limits,
\eta_{!}\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}

reflects effective descent morphisms.

Proof.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-functor such that
\eta_{!}F
is an effective descent
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
-functor. Since
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Set}
has fully faithful left and right adjoints, the same holds for
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Set}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
, since enrichment is a 2-functor which
preserves fully faithful functors by Lemma
3.7
.

Thus,
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Set}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
maps
\eta_{!}F
to a descent
functor, which, in turn, is reflected along
\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}

to its underlying function on objects, which must be surjective. Since
surjections are effective descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Set}
, we may apply
Proposition
2.6
to conclude that
F
is an effective
descent functor.
∎

Now, by applying Lucatelli Nunes’s criteria for the effective descent
morphisms (Theorem
2.12
) to Theorem

3.9
, we obtain (see also Lemma
3.5
):

Theorem 3.10

(
Prezado, 2024
, Theorem 3.3
)
.

If
\mathcal{V}
is a category with finite limits, and
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-functor such that

(I)

F\colon(\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1}))_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\to\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1})
is
an effective descent morphism,

(II)

F\times F\colon(\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}))_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\to\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})
is a descent morphism, and

(III)

F\times F\times F\colon(\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}))_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\to\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
is an almost descent morphism

in the category
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
for all
y_{0},\,y_{1},\,y_{2},\,y_{3}\in\mathcal{V}
,
then
F
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
.

Unlike Le Creurer’s and Lucatelli Nunes’s results, which describe the
effective descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
and
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
in terms of
morphisms in
\mathcal{V}
, the description we provide for the effective
descent morphisms of
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
(Theorem
3.10
) relies on
understanding (effective, almost) descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, prompting
their study.

3.3
Familial descent and effective descent morphisms

By Lemma
3.5
, we learn that when studying (effective, almost)
descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, it is enough to the consider covers
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
.

There is not much to say about (pullback-stable) epimorphisms in this
generality; they are, tautologically, the jointly epimorphic covers (preserved
by pullbacks).

Regarding (effective) descent morphisms, it is useful to compute the kernel
pair of a cover
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
; we use the
construction described in Proposition
3.1
. For each

j,k\in J
, we have a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}\times_{Y}\phi_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,j,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0,j,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{k}}

(3.5)

so the kernel pair of
\phi
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{j}\times_{Y}\phi_{k})_{j,k\in J\times J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},\delta_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0},\delta_{0})}

where
d_{i}\colon J\times J\to J
discards the
i
th component, for
i=0,\,1
.

We also define a category
\mathcal{D}_{J}
with set of objects
J+J^{2}
,
and for each
j,k\in J
, two different morphisms
(j,k)\to j
and
(j,k)\to k
.

For each cover
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
, we define a diagram
K_{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}_{J}\to\mathcal{V}
, mapping

–

(j,k)\to j
to
\delta_{1,j,k}\colon\phi_{j}\times_{Y}\phi_{k}\to X_{j}

–

(j,k)\to k
to
\delta_{0,j,k}\colon\phi_{j}\times_{Y}\phi_{k}\to X_{k}
,

We recall that in a category with finite limits, regular epimorphisms are the
coequalizers of their kernel pairs. With that in mind, we may obtain the
following result:

Lemma 3.11

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 4.1
)
.

A cover
\phi
is a (pullback-stable) regular epimorphism if and only if

\colim K_{\phi}\cong Y
(and the colimit is stable).

Proof.

We shall assume
J
is non-empty throughout. We have a natural
isomorphism

[\rightrightarrows,\Fam(\mathcal{V})](\ker\phi,\Delta_{(Z_{k})_{k\in K}})\cong\sum_{k\in K}[\mathcal{D}_{J},\mathcal{V}](K_{\phi},\Delta_{Z_{k}}),

and to verify this, note that the composite of
\ker\phi
with
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Set}
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\times J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

whose coequalizer is terminal. Since the data for a natural transformation

\ker\phi\to\Delta_{(Z_{k})_{k\in K}}
consists of a morphism
(f,\psi)\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to(Z_{k})_{k\in K}
such that

(f,\chi)\circ(d_{1},\delta_{1})=(f,\psi)\circ(d_{0},\delta_{0}),

there exists a unique
k\in K
such that
fj=k
for all
j\in J
, and
\chi_{j}\circ\delta_{1,i,j}=\chi_{i}\circ\delta_{0,i,j}

for all
i,j\in J
. This data corresponds to a unique natural
transformation
K_{\phi}\to\Delta_{Z_{k}}
.

So, if
K_{\phi}
has a colimit, then

\displaystyle\Fam(\mathcal{V})(\colim K_{\phi},(Z_{k})_{k\in K})

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{k\in K}\mathcal{V}(\colim K_{\phi},Z_{k})

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{k\in K}[\mathcal{D}_{J},\mathcal{V}](K_{\phi},\Delta_{Z_{k}})

\displaystyle\cong[\rightrightarrows,\Fam(\mathcal{V})](\ker\phi,\Delta_{(Z_{k})_{k\in K}}),

hence
\ker\phi
has a colimit and
\colim\ker\phi\cong\colim K_{\phi}
. Conversely, if
\ker\phi
has a colimit, then its underlying
set is the coequalizer of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\times J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

which is the terminal object, hence
\ker\phi
is connected and can be
identified with an object of
\mathcal{V}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\colim(\ker\phi),Z)

\displaystyle\cong\Fam(\mathcal{V})(\colim(\ker\phi),Z)

\displaystyle\cong[\rightrightarrows,\Fam(\mathcal{V})](\ker\phi,\Delta_{Z})

\displaystyle\cong[\mathcal{D}_{J},\mathcal{V}](K_{\phi},\Delta_{Z}),

so we conclude that
\colim\ker\phi\cong\colim K_{\phi}
.

If the colimit of
K_{\phi}
is stable under pullback, for each
j\in J
we write

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}^{*}(\omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}

for the pullback of
\phi_{j}
and a morphism
\omega\colon Z\to Y

for each
j\in J
, so that
Z\cong\colim K_{\psi}
, hence
Z\cong\colim\ker\psi
. So, if we have a morphism
\xi\colon(W_{l})_{l\in L}\to Y
, we can do this procedure for
\xi_{l}
for each
l\in L
,
and then take the coproduct of the results, confirming the pullback
stability of the colimit of
\ker\phi
.

Conversely, if the colimit of
\ker\phi
is stable under pullback, for
any morphism
\omega\colon Z\to Y
, we may consider the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}^{*}(\omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}

and since
Y\cong\colim\ker\psi\cong\colim K_{\psi}
, we immediately
conclude that the colimit of
K_{\phi}
is stable under pullback.
∎

To understand effective descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, we recall that
lax descent data for a cover
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
consists
of

–

a morphism
(p,\pi)\colon(W_{k})_{k\in K}\to(X_{j})_{j\in J}
in

\Fam(\mathcal{V})
,

–

and a morphism
(\gamma,\Gamma)\colon D_{1}^{*}(p,\pi)\to D_{0}^{*}(p,\pi)

in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\downarrow(\phi_{i}\times_{Y}\phi_{j})_{i,j\in J\times J}

satisfying reflexivity (
2.1
) and transitivity
(
2.2
) conditions, namely,

–

\nu_{0}=S_{0}^{*}(\gamma,\Gamma)\circ\nu_{1}
,

–

\theta_{01}\circ D_{1}^{*}(\gamma,\Gamma)\circ\theta_{12}=D_{0}^{*}(\gamma,\Gamma)\circ\theta_{02}\circ D_{2}^{*}(\gamma,\Gamma)
,

Since
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Set}
preserves pullbacks, we recover descent data
(p,\pi)
for the unique morphism
J\to 1
, implying that
K\cong I\times J
for some set
I
, and, under this isomorphism, we have
p\cong d_{0}\colon I\times J\to J
. Moreover, we note that the underlying
\mathsf{Set}
-pullbacks of
D_{i}\colon(\phi_{j}\times_{Y}\phi_{k})_{j,k\in J\times J}\to(X_{j})_{j\in J}
for
i=0,1
are given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\times J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\times J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\times K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\times J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

and since
(\mathsf{id}\times p)\circ\gamma=p\times\mathsf{id}
, we deduce that
\gamma
is isomorphic to the function

\displaystyle I\times J\times J

\displaystyle\to J\times I\times J

\displaystyle(i,j,k)

\displaystyle\mapsto(j,i,k),

and the reflexivity and transitivity conditions are given in components by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{ij}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}_{0,j}\delta_{1,j,j}^{*}(\pi_{i,j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}_{0,j}(\delta_{0,j,j}^{*}(\pi_{i,j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{1,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{0,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}_{0,j}(\Gamma_{i,j,j})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,j,k,l}^{*}(\delta_{1,j,l}^{*}(\pi_{i,j}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,j,k,l}^{*}(\delta_{0,j,l}^{*}(\pi_{i,l}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,j,k,l}^{*}(\delta_{1,j,l}^{*}(\pi_{i,j}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0,j,k,l}^{*}(\delta_{0,j,l}^{*}(\pi_{i,l}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,j,k,l}^{*}(\delta_{0,j,l}^{*}(\pi_{i,k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{0,j,k,l}^{*}(\delta_{1,j,l}^{*}(\pi_{i,k}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,j,k,l}^{*}(\Gamma_{i,j,l})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{01,j,k,l}(\pi_{i,l})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{12,j,k,l}(\pi_{i,j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,j,k,l}^{*}(\Gamma_{i,j,k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{02,j,k,l}(\pi_{i,k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{*}_{0,j,k,l}(\Gamma_{i,k,l})}

(3.6)

for each
i\in I
,
j,k,l\in J
. This observation allows us to prove
the following result:

Lemma 3.12

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 4.2
)
.

Let
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
be a cover in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
. We
have an equivalence

\mathsf{Desc}(\phi)\simeq\Fam(\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathsf{conn}}(\phi)),

(3.7)

where
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathsf{conn}}(\phi)
is the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Desc}(\phi)

consisting of the connected objects.

Proof.

If
(p,\pi)\colon(W_{i,j})_{i,j\in I\times J}\to(X_{j})_{j\in J}

and
(\gamma,\Gamma)
is a lax descent datum for
\phi
as given
above, then for each
i\in I
, we define
W_{i,-}=(W_{i,j})_{j\in J}
, as well as a morphism
(\iota_{i},\mathsf{id})\colon W_{i,-}\to(W_{i,j})_{i,j\in I\times J}
, where
\iota_{i}(j)=(i,j)
.

We note that the composites
(p,\pi)\circ(\iota_{i},\mathsf{id})=(\mathsf{id},\pi_{i,-})
, and
(\mathsf{id},\Gamma_{i,-.-})\colon D_{1}^{*}(\mathsf{id},\pi_{i,-})\to D_{0}^{*}(\mathsf{id},\pi_{i,-})
constitute a lax descent datum for
\phi
, for
each
i\in I
. Indeed, Diagrams (
3.3
) and
(
3.6
) commute for each fixed
i\in I
, confirming
reflexivity and transitivity for each component.

Thus, the lax descent datum
(p,\pi)
,
(\gamma,\Gamma)
is the
coproduct of the lax descent data
(\mathsf{id},\pi_{i,-})
,
(\mathsf{id},\Gamma_{i,-})
for each
i\in I
.

Now, we let

(\mathsf{id},\xi)\colon(V_{j})_{j\in J}\to(X_{j})_{j\in J},\qquad(\mathsf{id},\Xi)\colon(\mathsf{id},\delta^{*}_{1}\circ\xi)\to(\mathsf{id},\delta^{*}_{0}\circ\xi)

be a connected lax descent datum, and let
(g,\chi)\colon(V_{j})_{j\in J}\to(W_{i,j})_{i,j\in I\times J}
be a morphism such that
(d_{0},\pi)\circ(g,\chi)=(\mathsf{id},\xi)
and the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}_{1}(\mathsf{id},\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}_{0}(\mathsf{id},\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}_{1}(d_{0},\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}_{0}(d_{0},\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}_{1}(g,\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id},\Xi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}_{0}(g,\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma,\Gamma)}

commutes. Since
p\circ g=\mathsf{id}
, we conclude that
g(j)=(h(j),j)
for a function
h\colon J\to I
, and since
\gamma(h(j),j,k)=(j,h(k),k)
for all
j,k\in J
, we conclude that
h
is constant;
let
i\in I
be its value, so that
g=\iota_{i}
. We obtain a
factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{i,j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{i,j})_{i,j\in I\times J},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id},\chi_{i,-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{i},\mathsf{id})}

so, we may apply Proposition
3.4
to conclude our proof.
∎

In fact, by noticing that the underlying objects, morphisms and properties for
each connected descent datum lie in a slice category of a fiber of
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Set}
, we deduce that:

Corollary 3.13
.

The category
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathsf{conn}}(\phi)
is the lax descent category of the
following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{J}\downarrow(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{J\times J}\downarrow(\phi_{i}\times\phi_{j})_{i,j\in J\times J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{J\times J\times J}\downarrow(\phi_{i}\times_{Y}\phi_{j}\times_{Y}\phi_{k})_{i,j,k\in J\times J\times J}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{0}^{*}}

(3.8)

Remark 3.14
.

This corollary provides us with another point of view; let
\mathcal{D}^{+}_{J}

be the category whose set of objects is given by
J+J^{2}+J^{3}
,
containing
\mathcal{D}_{J}
as a subcategory, such that for each triple
i,j,k\in J
, we have three distinct morphisms
(i,j,k)\to(j,k)
,
(i,j,k)\to(i,k)
and
(i,j,k)\to(i,j)
, such that the following
diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

and for each
j\in J
, a morphism
j\to(j,j)
such that both of
the composites below are the identity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

We define a diagram
K^{+}_{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}^{+}_{J}\to\mathcal{V}
, extending

K_{\phi}
, where
(i,j,k)\mapsto\phi_{i}\times_{Y}\phi_{j}\times_{Y}\phi_{k}
, and the morphisms from objects in
J^{3}
to
J^{2}
are
mapped to the respective projections, while the morphisms from
J
to
J^{2}
are mapped to the respective diagonals
\sigma_{0,i}\colon X_{i}\to\phi_{i}\times_{Y}\phi_{i}
. It can be shown that the 2-limit of the
composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{D}^{+}_{J})^{\mathsf{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K^{+}_{\phi})^{\mathsf{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\downarrow-}

is equivalent to
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathsf{conn}}(\phi)
; by taking products of categories,
we recover Diagram (
3.8
).

Theorem 3.15

(
Prezado, 2024
, Theorem 4.3
)
.

Let
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
be a cover in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

\phi
is an effective descent morphism.

(ii)

We have an equivalence
\mathcal{V}\downarrow Y\simeq\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathsf{conn}}(\phi)
.

Proof.

First, we observe that
\Fam(\mathcal{V}\downarrow Y)\simeq\Fam(\mathcal{V})\downarrow Y
,
since for any morphism
\phi\colon(W_{j})_{i\in J}\to Y
, we have
\phi=\prod_{j\in J}\phi_{j}
as objects in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})\downarrow Y
, and
if we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

then there exists a unique
j\in J
(given by
f
) factoring
(f,\omega)=(\iota_{j},\mathsf{id})\circ\omega
uniquely, so we may apply
Theorem
3.4
.

Since the comparison functor
\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(\phi)}\colon\Fam(\mathcal{V})\downarrow Y\to\mathsf{Desc}(\phi)
preserves connected objects, we obtain an equivalence

\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(\phi)}\simeq\Fam(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{conn}}^{\Ker(\phi)}),

(3.9)

where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{conn}}^{\Ker(\phi)}\colon\mathcal{V}\downarrow Y\to\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathsf{conn}}(\phi)
is the restriction of
\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(\phi)}
to
the connected objects.

We have
(i)

\implies

(ii)
,
since we have (
3.9
), and
\Fam
reflects equivalences,
as the unit is 2-cartesian. Conversely,
(ii)

\implies

(i)
follows immediately by
(
3.9
).
∎

3.4
Examples

The study of descent morphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
for certain categories
\mathcal{V}
with finite limits inspired us to highlight the following specialization of
Lemma
3.11
:

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
be a cover in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
such
that for all
j\in J
,
\phi_{j}
is a monomorphism; that is,
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
is a family of subobjects of
Y
. If the kernel pair
of
\phi
has a coequalizer, then
\colim K_{\phi}\cong\bigvee_{j\in J}X_{j}
as a subobject of
Y
, where
K_{\phi}
is given as in Lemma

3.11
.

Proof.

Let
\xi\colon\colim K_{\phi}\to Y
be the unique morphism such that
\phi_{j}=\xi\circ q_{j}
for all
j\in J
, where
q\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to\colim K_{\phi}
is the coequalizer, which is pullback-stable by
hypothesis.

It is enough to prove that
\xi
is a monomorphism. We consider the
following diagram in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j}\land X_{k})_{j,k\in J\times J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi^{*}_{0}(X_{j}))_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi^{*}_{1}(X_{j}))_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\times_{Y}\xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim K_{\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{j})_{j\in J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim K_{\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}|_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}|_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

whose squares are pullbacks. Since
q
is pullback-stable, it follows
that
(X_{j}\land X_{k})_{j,k\in J\times J}\to(\pi^{*}_{i}(X_{j}))_{j\in J}

is a regular epimorphism for
i=0,1
. Its kernel pair is the kernel
pair of
(X_{j}\land X_{k})_{j,k\in J\times J}\to(X_{j})_{j\in J}
,
hence
\pi_{i}|_{j}\colon\pi^{*}_{i}(X_{j})\cong X_{j}
is an isomorphism for all

j\in J
and
i=0,1
. We observe that
q^{*}
is conservative,
so
\pi_{i}
is an isomorphism for
i=0,1
. But
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}
is
the kernel pair of
\xi
, so it must be a monomorphism.

If
W
is a subobject of
Y
such that
X_{j}\leqslant W
for all
j
,
then the above observation (with
W
replacing
Y
) also confirms that
\colim K_{\phi}\leqslant W
. Thus,
\colim K_{\phi}\cong\bigvee_{j}X_{j}
in the
(thin) category of subobjects of
Y
.
∎

Thus, if a cover
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
of monomorphisms
is a descent morphism in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, we conclude that
Y\cong\bigvee_{j\in J}X_{j}
, a perspective that is helpful when
\mathcal{V}
is thin or regular.

3.4.1
Meet semilattices

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a thin category. A morphism
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
is the assertion that “for all
j\in J
, we have

X_{j}\leqslant Y
”. Therefore, we simply write
(X_{j})_{j\in J}\leqslant Y
in
this setting.

A thin category
\mathcal{V}
is said to be a
meet semilattice with a
top element
if
\mathcal{V}
is a thin category with finite limits, which are
called
(finite) meets
in this context.

Lemma 3.17

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 4.4
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a meet semilattice with a top element, and let
(X_{j})_{j\in J}\leqslant Y
be a cover.

(a)

It is an epimorphism if and only if
J
is non-empty.

(b)

If it is an epimorphism, then it is pullback-stable.

(c)

It is a regular epimorphism if and only if
\bigvee_{j\in J}X_{j}\cong Y
.

(d)

It is a pullback-stable regular epimorphism if and only if we have

Z\cong\bigvee_{j\in J}Z\wedge X_{j}.

(3.10)

for all
Z\leqslant Y
.

(e)

If it is a descent morphism, and
\mathcal{V}\downarrow Y
is (co)complete,
then it is an effective descent morphism if, and only if, for every
family
(W_{j})_{j\in J}
with
W_{j}\leqslant X_{j}
for all
j\in J
, satisfying

W_{j}\wedge X_{i}\cong X_{j}\wedge W_{i}

for every pair
i,j\in J
, we have

X_{j}\wedge\bigvee_{i\in J}W_{i}\cong W_{j}

for all
j\in J
.

Proof.

If
(X_{j})_{j\in J}\leqslant Y
, then for all
j\in J
,
X_{j}\leqslant Y
is an epimorphism. Thus, we conclude that
(X_{j})_{j\in J}\leqslant Y
is
an epimorphism if and only if the underlying function
J\to 1
is an
epimorphism, which is the case if and only if
J
is non-empty,
confirming
(a)
.

If
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
is an epimorphism, then for all
Z\leqslant Y
, we
have
(X_{j}\wedge Z)_{j\in J}\leqslant Z
, which is still an epimorphism, as

J
is non-empty, proving
(b)
.

We also have
(c)
as a consequence of Lemma

3.11
, and the condition for pullback-stability is
precisely (
3.10
), giving
(d)
.

Let
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\leqslant Y
be a pullback-stable regular
epimorphism. We have that
\phi
is an effective descent morphism if and
only if
\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(\phi)}_{\mathsf{conn}}\colon\mathcal{V}\downarrow Y\to\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathsf{conn}}(\phi)
is essentially surjective, by
Theorem
3.15
.

We highlight that connected descent data for
\phi
consists of a family
of subobjects
W_{j}\leqslant X_{j}
indexed by
j\in J
such that
W_{j}\land X_{i}\cong X_{j}\land W_{i}
for all
i,j\in J
; the reflexivity and
transitivity conditions are automatically satisfied, as
\mathcal{V}
is
thin.

Thus,
\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(\phi)}_{\mathsf{conn}}
is essentially surjective if and
only if for all connected descent data
(W_{j})_{j\in J}\leqslant(X_{j})_{j\in J}
, there exists
Z\leqslant Y
such that
X_{j}\wedge Z\cong W_{j}
.
Given that
\mathcal{V}\downarrow Y
is (co)complete, we let
Z\cong\bigvee_{j\in J}W_{j}
. We have

X_{j}\land Z\cong\bigvee_{i\in J}X_{j}\land W_{i}\cong\bigvee_{i\in J}W_{j}\land X_{i}\cong W_{j},

which concludes our proof.
∎

A bounded meet semilattice
\mathcal{V}
is said to be a
Heyting
lattice
2
2

2

Also known as
implicative semilattices

Nemitz, 1965

and
Brouwerian semilattices

Köhler, 1981
.
if
\mathcal{V}
is
cartesian closed; that is, the functor
A\wedge-
has a right adjoint
functor for all objects
A
. As a corollary, we obtain:

Corollary 3.18

(
Prezado, 2024
, Corollary 4.5
)
.

If
\mathcal{V}
is a Heyting semilattice, regular epimorphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
are pullback-stable.

Proof.

Since
Z\wedge-
preserves joins, (
3.10
) is always
satisfied.
∎

Corollary 3.19
.

If
\mathcal{V}
is a (co)complete lattice, pullback-stable regular
epimorphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
are effective for descent.

Proof.

For all
Y
,
\mathcal{V}\downarrow Y
is cocomplete.
∎

Combining both of the previous results yields:

Corollary 3.20

(
Prezado, 2024
, Corollary 4.6
)
.

If
\mathcal{V}
is a (co)complete Heyting (semi)lattice, regular
epimorphisms in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
are effective for descent.

So, we recover one implication of
Clementino & Hofmann, 2004
, Theorem 5.4
:

Theorem 3.21

(
Prezado, 2024
, Theorem 4.7
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a (co)complete Heyting lattice, and let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-functor. If

\bigvee_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\cong\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})

(3.11)

for all
y_{0},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
, then
F
is an effective descent

\mathcal{V}
-functor.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
(II)
is satisfied, and since
F
is
surjective on objects (consider (
3.11
) with
y_{0}=y_{1}=y_{2}
), it follows by Lemma
3.17
that condition

(III)
holds. Moreover, if we consider
(
3.11
) with
y_{1}=y_{2}
, so that
\mathcal{D}(y_{1},y_{2})\cong 1
, we have

\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1})\cong\bigvee_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\leqslant\bigvee_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1}),

and since
\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1})\leqslant\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1})
for all
x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}
, we conclude that
(\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1}))_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\leqslant\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1})
is a regular epimorphism in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, which is effective
for descent by Corollary
3.20
, guaranteeing

(I)
. Thus, Theorem
3.10
can be
applied to conclude that
F
is effective for descent.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

As alluded to in the Introduction, Theorem
3.10
confirms
the link between the idea of “chain-surjectivity” conditions of

Le, 1999
and the “chain-surjectivity” of
Janelidze & Sobral, 2002
, as evidenced by
Theorem
3.21
.

3.4.2
Regular categories

The same ideas work here, if we employ the (regular epi, mono)-factorization
system of a regular category.

Lemma 3.23

(
Prezado, 2024
, Lemma 4.8
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a regular category, and let
\phi\colon(X_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
be a cover. For each
j\in J
, we consider the (regular
epi,mono)-factorization of
\phi_{j}
, given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{j}}

(3.12)

where
\pi_{j}
is a descent morphism, and
\iota_{j}
is a
monomorphism for all
j\in J
. Thus, we consider the cover
\iota\colon(M_{j})_{j\in J}\to Y
.

(a)

\phi
is a (pullback-stable) epimorphism if and only if
\iota

is a (pullback-stable) epimorphism.

(b)

\phi
is a (pullback-stable) regular epimorphism if and only if
\iota
is a (pullback-stable) regular epimorphism.

(c)

If
\pi_{j}
is an effective descent morphism for all
j\in J
,
then
\phi
is effective for descent if and only if
\iota

is effective for descent.

Proof.

The factorization (
3.12
) gives a factorization
\phi=\iota\circ(\mathsf{id},\pi)
in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
, and since
\pi_{j}
is a descent
morphism for all
j
,
(\mathsf{id},\pi)
is a coproduct of descent
morphisms, therefore it is a descent morphism in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
. Thus, we
obtain
(a)
and
(b)
by composition and
cancellation
Janelidze et al., 2004
, Propositions 1.3 and 1.5
.

Moreover, if
\pi_{j}
is effective for descent for all
j\in J
, then
so is
(\mathsf{id},\pi)
, and
(c)
follows by
Janelidze & Tholen, 1997
, Theorem
4.5
, as the basic bifibration respects the BED (see
Janelidze & Tholen, 1997
, 4.4,
4.6
).
∎

Thus, the study of (effective) descent covers in
\Fam(\mathcal{V})
can be reduced
to the study of (effective) descent covers of monomorphisms (and effective
descent morphisms in
\mathcal{V}
). When applied to the study of effective
descent morphisms in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
, we obtain:

Theorem 3.24

(
Prezado, 2024
, Theorem 4.9
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a regular category, let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}

be a
\mathcal{V}
-functor, and consider the hom-covers

\displaystyle F=(F_{x_{0},x_{1}})_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1}))_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\to\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1})

\displaystyle F\times F=(F_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}})_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}))_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\to\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle F\times F\times F=(F_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}})_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathcal{C}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}))_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}\to\mathcal{D}(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})

and their respective (regular epi, mono)-factorizations

\displaystyle F_{x_{0},x_{1}}

\displaystyle=I_{x_{0},x_{1}}\circ P_{x_{0},x_{1}},

\displaystyle F_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}}

\displaystyle=I_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}}\circ P_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}},

\displaystyle F_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}}

\displaystyle=I_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}}\circ P_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}}.

If

(a)

P_{x_{0},x_{1}}
is an effective descent morphism for all
x_{0},x_{1}
,

(b)

(I_{x_{0},x_{1}})_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}
is an effective descent
morphism,

(c)

(I_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}})_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}
is a descent morphism, and

(d)

(I_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}})_{x_{i}\in F^{*}y_{i}}
is an almost descent
morphism,

then
F
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.23
,

–

conditions
(a)
and
(b)

together guarantee
(I)
,

–

conditions
(c)
and
(d)
respectively
guarantee
(II)
and
(III)
,

so that Theorem
3.10
can be applied.
∎

The above list of conditions can be further reduced if
\mathcal{V}
satisfies
extra properties. For instance, if
\mathcal{V}
is Barr-exact, or locally
cartesian closed, then descent morphisms are effective descent morphisms, so
condition
(a)
is redundant.

More specifically, a
\mathsf{CHaus}
-functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}

satisfying
(b)
,
(c)
and

(d)
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathsf{CHaus}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
, as
\mathsf{CHaus}
is an Barr-exact category
Marra & Reggio, 2020
. Moreover, it
is shown therein that
\mathsf{Stn}
is a regular category, thus if a
\mathsf{Stn}
-functor satisfies all of the hypotheses of Theorem
3.24
,
then it is an effective descent
\mathsf{Stn}
-functor.

Chapter 4
Generalized internal multicategory functors

A
multicategory
is a categorical structure which models the notion of
“multimorphisms”: morphisms which map from a (possibly empty) finite string
of inputs to a single output. The quintessential example of such a structure
is the multicategory
\mathsf{Vect}
of vector spaces over some field
\mathbb{F}
, and
multilinear maps
. A multilinear map
f\colon(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\to W
has a finite string
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
of vector spaces as
the domain, and a vector space
W
as codomain. It consists of a function

f\colon V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{n}\to W

which is linear in each component:

f(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i}+\lambda w,\ldots,v_{n})=f(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i},\ldots,v_{n})+\lambda f(v_{1},\ldots,w,\ldots,v_{n}),

where
v_{j}\in V_{j}
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
w\in V_{i}
for
each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
. In case
n=0
, a multilinear map
f\colon()\to W
is simply a vector
f\in W
, or equivalently, a linear map
f\colon\mathbb{F}\to W
. In case
n=1
, a multilinear map
f\colon(V_{1})\to W
is an ordinary linear
map.

As is the case with categories, multicategories also have an adequate

composition operation
. In the case of
\mathsf{Vect}
, if we have a
finite string of multilinear maps
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
given by

g_{j}\colon(U_{j1},\ldots,U_{jk_{j}})\to V_{j},

then we have the composite multilinear map

f\circ(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\colon(U_{11},\ldots,U_{1k_{1}},\ldots,U_{n1},\ldots,U_{nk_{n}})\to W

whose underlying function is given by
f\circ(g_{1}\times\ldots\times g_{n})
. Of course, the identity linear map
\mathsf{id}_{W}\colon(W)\to W
is a
(multi)linear map, and these satisfy suitable associativity and unit laws.
Naturally, if we consider the multilinear maps whose domain is a string of
length 1, we precisely recover the ordinary category of vector spaces and
linear maps.

The notion of multicategory can be traced back to
Lambék, 1969
, p. 103
, where
it was developed for the purpose of studying deductive systems in logic, and
it has since found applications in algebraic topology and higher category
theory. A comprehensive introduction to these categorical structures is given
in
Leinster, 2004
.

The composition operation of
\mathsf{Vect}
carries an underlying structure on
the domains, given by
concatenation
of strings, as does the identity
multilinear map,
casting
each vector space as a string of vector
spaces of length 1. These operations are well modeled by the multiplication
and unit natural transformations for the
free monoid monad

(-)^{*}
on
\mathsf{Set}
. Indeed, the multicategory of vector spaces may be described
by a span of functions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{set of vector spaces}\}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{multilinear maps}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{set of vector spaces}\}}
domain
codomain

and the identity and composition operation, as well as the associativity and
unit laws, can be described diagrammatically as well, via the monad structure
of
(-)^{*}
, and its properties. Let
\mathsf{Vect}_{0}
be the set of vector
spaces, and
\mathsf{Vect}_{1}
be the set of multilinear maps. We obtain the set
\mathsf{Vect}_{2}
of “multicomposable” pairs of multilinear maps via the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
domain
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{codomain}^{*}}

so that the composition operation is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}}
composition
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{domain}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{codomain}^{*}}
domain
codomain
concatenation
domain
codomain

and the identity maps are given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Vect}_{0}}
cast
identity
domain
codomain

This diagrammatic description lends itself to the “internalization” of the
notion of multicategory to any category
\mathcal{V}
with pullbacks, provided
we also replace the free monoid monad on
\mathsf{Set}
by an arbitrary monad

T=(T,m,e)
on
\mathcal{V}
. Incidentally, this also allows for the
“shape” of the domain to be more general than “finite strings”. Indeed,
these ideas gave rise to
T
-catégories
, first defined in

Burroni, 1971
, and later studied by
Hermida, 2000
when
T
is a

cartesian
monad. In these works, generalized multicategories are
defined to be monads in the bicategory
\mathsf{Span}_{T}(\mathcal{V})
.

The main theme of this thesis is to obtain a unified perspective on the
effective descent morphisms in generalized categorical structures, and the
purpose of this chapter, covering the work done in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, is to provide
an understanding of these morphisms in the category
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
of

T-categories
internal to
\mathcal{V}
. We will undertake two
approaches.

Our first approach to the study of effective descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})

can be summed up in four steps:

–

we construct
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
as a 2-equalizer of a diagram of categories of
essentially algebraic theories internal to
\mathcal{V}
,

–

we recall from
Le, 1999
, Proposition 3.2.4
that effective descent
morphisms in essentially algebraic theories internal to
\mathcal{V}
can
be described via descent conditions on the underlying data,

–

we recall the description of effective descent morphisms of a
pseudoequalizer (isoinserter) via Proposition
2.7
,

–

we confirm that the embedding of
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
into the associated
pseudoequalizer reflects effective descent morphisms.

Section
4.1
illustrates the tools and techniques used for
the construction of
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
in a simpler
T
-structure, that of

reflexive

T
-graphs
, and the full construction is carried
out in Section
4.2
. Afterwards, Section
4.3
is
devoted to confirming that effective descent morphisms are reflected along the
embedding of
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
into the associated pseudoequalizer.

In Section
4.4
, we provide a second method to obtain a
description of the effective descent functors of
T
-categories. Here, we
employ the ideas of
Le, 1999
to extend his results to our setting, by
directly studying the “sketch” of these generalized multicategories.

4.1
Reflexive
T
-graphs

Let
T=(T,m,e)
be a monad on a category
\mathcal{V}
with pullbacks. For
the purpose of studying effective descent functors of
T
-categories, we
obtain sharper results by describing
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
as a 2 - equalizer of a
suitable diagram of categories. Before providing such a description, we will
first consider a simpler
T
-structure as a guiding example.

For a pointed endofunctor
T=(T,e)
on
\mathcal{V}
, a
reflexive

T
-graph

x
consists of

–

an object
x_{0}
of
objects
,

–

an object
x_{1}
of
arrows
,

–

a
domain
morphism
d_{1}\colon x_{1}\to Tx_{0}
,

–

a
codomain
morphism
d_{0}\colon x_{1}\to x_{0}
,

–

a
loop
morphism
s_{0}\colon x_{0}\to x_{1}
,

which must satisfy
d_{0}\circ s_{0}=\mathsf{id}
and
d_{1}\circ s_{0}=e
. We
note that this data can be organized in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

(4.1)

Moreover, a morphism of reflexive
T
-graphs
f\colon x\to y

consists of

–

an
object
morphism
f_{0}\colon x_{0}\to y_{0}
,

–

an
arrow
morphism
f_{1}\colon x_{1}\to y_{1}
,

satisfying
d_{0}\circ f_{1}=f_{0}\circ d_{0}
,
d_{1}\circ f_{1}=Tf_{0}\circ d_{1}
and
f_{1}\circ s_{0}=s_{0}\circ f_{0}
. These form a category
\mathsf{RGrph}(T,\mathcal{V})
, with componentwise composition and identities. We observe that
these are the
T-graphes pointés
of
Burroni, 1971
.

We take this opportunity to remark that reflexive
T
-graphs allow us to
draw conclusions about the descent theory of categorical structures:

Lemma 4.1

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma A.3
)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a class of epimorphisms in
\mathsf{RGrph}(T,\mathcal{V})
such that

–

\mathcal{E}
contains all split epimorphisms,

–

\mathcal{E}
is closed under composition,

–

if
g\circ f,f\in\mathcal{E}
, then
g\in\mathcal{E}
(right
cancellation),

and let
f\colon x\to y
be a morphism of reflexive
T
-graphs. If

f_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
, then
f_{0}\in\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

In any reflexive
T
-graph
x
, the codomain morphism
d_{0}\colon x_{1}\to x_{0}
is a split epimorphism since
d_{0}\circ s_{0}=\mathsf{id}
, so that

d_{0}\in\mathcal{E}
. If
f_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
, then we have
d_{0}\circ f_{1}=f_{0}\circ d_{0}\in\mathcal{E}
by closure under composition, and
f_{0}\in\mathcal{E}
by right cancellation.
∎

Of particular interest are the classes
\mathcal{E}
given by the (effective,
almost) descent morphisms, which satisfy each of the properties. Taking
T=\mathsf{id}
, we observe that the condition that
p_{0}
is an effective descent
morphism is redundant in Theorem
2.10
, and can be omitted.

Returning to our main point, we observe that the category
\mathsf{RGrph}(T,\mathcal{V})
can
be described by a (2-)equalizer of diagram categories as well: we consider the
graph

\mathcal{G}=\hbox to81.63pt{\vbox to52.93pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.81245pt\lower-23.73116pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

with relations
d_{1}\circ s_{0}=e_{0}
and
d_{0}\circ s_{0}=\mathsf{id}
, and we
consider the diagram category
[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V}]
, together with
functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2,\mathcal{V}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}^{*}}

induced by the inclusions
x_{0}\to\mathcal{G}
and
(x_{0}\xrightarrow{e_{0}}y_{0})\to\mathcal{G}
, where
2=(\cdot\to\cdot)
.

We recall that any natural transformation
\phi\colon F\to G
of
functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is precisely determined by a functor
\phi^{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{C}\to[2,\mathcal{D}]
, which satisfies
\mathsf{ev}_{0}\circ\phi^{\sharp}=F
and
\mathsf{ev}_{1}\circ\phi^{\sharp}=G
, where
\mathsf{ev}_{j}\colon[\mathcal{J},\mathcal{D}]\to\mathcal{D}
is the evaluation functor.

For example, if we take the point
e\colon\mathsf{id}\to T
of the endofunctor

T
,
e^{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{V}\to[2,\mathcal{V}]
is a functor which
satisfies
\mathsf{ev}_{0}\circ e^{\sharp}=\mathsf{id}
and
\mathsf{ev}_{1}\circ e^{\sharp}=T
.
With this notation, we obtain the following statement:

Lemma 4.2
.

We have a 2-equalizer diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{RGrph}(T,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2,\mathcal{V}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{*}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\sharp}\circ x_{0}^{*}}

(4.2)

Proof.

From the condition imposed by the 2-equalizer in
\mathsf{CAT}
we obtain the
full subcategory of
[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V}]
whose diagrams are of the form
(
4.1
).
∎

4.2
Internal
T
-categories

Let
T=(T,m,e)
be a monad on a category
\mathcal{V}
with finite limits.
Recall that
T
is said to be
cartesian
if
T
preserves
pullbacks and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{y}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTy}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{y}}

are pullback squares for all
f\colon x\to y
.

The category
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
of
T-categories
was defined
diagrammatically in
Burroni, 1971
, I.1
for general monads
T
, and this is
the definition we will use throughout this chapter. However, Burroni also
observed that the category of
T
-categories can equivalently be defined as
the category of monads for the (proarrow) equipment
\mathsf{Span}_{T}(\mathcal{V})
of

T
-spans
, for
T
a cartesian monad; indeed, this is precisely
how
T
-categories were defined in
Hermida, 2000
.

Here, we shall verify that
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
can be given via a 2-equalizer
involving the category of
\mathcal{V}
-models for a finite limit sketch
\mathcal{S}
. Its underlying graph is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
