\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Moreover, if
T
is cartesian, then
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
has pullbacks, and the
inclusion
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{V})
preserves them.

Proof.

The objects of the 2-equalizer are precisely those diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTx_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTx_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{x_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{x_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ts_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{x_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{x_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTd_{0}}

(4.6)

satisfying the relations imposed by
\mathcal{S}
, such that the following
squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTx_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTx_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTd_{0}}

(4.7)

are pullback diagrams; compare (
4.6
) with
Burroni, 1971
, Figure
1
. Since
T
preserves pullbacks, the rightmost pullback diagram
in (
4.7
) can be replaced by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTx_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTx_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTd_{0}}

When
T
is cartesian, both
d^{1}
and
d^{0}
preserve pullbacks,
hence (
4.5
) can be seen as a 2-equalizer in the category of
categories with pullbacks and pullback-preserving functors.
∎

4.3
Effective descent morphisms via bilimits

Let
T=(T,m,e)
be a cartesian monad on a category
\mathcal{V}
with
pullbacks. We begin by observing that:

Lemma 4.5

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma A.1
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a pullback-stable class of morphisms of
\mathcal{V}
.

T
creates such morphisms in its essential image.

Proof.

Let
f
be a morphism such that
Tf\in\mathcal{P}
. Since the
naturality squares for
m
and
e
at
f
are pullbacks, we conclude
that
TTf,\,f\in\mathcal{P}
.
∎

We now consider the sketch
\mathcal{S}
constructed in Section

4.2
.

Lemma 4.6

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Proposition 4.2
)
.

Let
p\colon x\to y
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{V})
. If

–

p_{0},p^{\prime}_{0},p^{\prime\prime}_{0},p_{1},p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime\prime}_{1}
are effective descent
morphisms,

–

p_{2},p^{\prime}_{2}
are descent morphisms, and

–

p_{3}
is an almost descent morphism,

then
p
is an effective descent morphism.

Proof.

The proof rests in describing
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{V})
as the category of
\mathcal{V}
-models for a suitable essentially algebraic theory; then the result is an
immediate consequence of
Le, 1999
, Proposition 3.2.4
.

Indeed, we obtain a sketch
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
, Morita equivalent to
\mathcal{S}
, from the essentially algebraic theory
\mathcal{A}
defined by

–

sorts
x_{0},\,x_{1},\,x^{\prime}_{0},\,x^{\prime}_{1},\,x^{\prime\prime}_{0},\,x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,

–

total operations given by the arrows of the full subgraph of the
underlying graph of
\mathcal{S}
consisting of the aforementioned sorts,

–

partial operations given by
d_{1}\colon x_{1}\times x^{\prime}_{1}\to x_{1}
,
d^{\prime}_{1}\colon x^{\prime}_{1}\times x^{\prime\prime}_{1}\to x^{\prime}_{1}
, and the limit cones of
\mathcal{S}
, with
x_{2}
and
x^{\prime}_{2}
respectively replaced by
x_{1}\times x^{\prime}_{1}
and
x^{\prime}_{1}\times x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, give the equations for these
partial operations,

–

the remaining equations are given by the underlying relations of
\mathcal{S}
, with
x_{2}
,
x_{3}
and
x^{\prime}_{2}
replaced by
x_{1}\times x^{\prime}_{1}
,
x_{1}\times x^{\prime}_{1}\times x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
x^{\prime}_{1}\times x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,
respectively.

The sketch
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
constructed from
\mathcal{A}
via the
procedure described in
Le, 1999
, 3.2.1
contains
\mathcal{S}
as a
“subsketch”, containing extra limit cones for the formal products
x_{1}\times x^{\prime}_{1}
,
x^{\prime}_{1}\times x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
x_{1}\times x^{\prime}_{1}\times x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, which are used to construct the limit cones of
x_{2},\,x^{\prime}_{2}
and
x_{3}
, as well as the (derived) partial operations and equations.

∎

Denoting by
\PsEq(F,G)
the
pseudoequalizer
of a pair of
functors
F,G\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, we obtain:

Lemma 4.7

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma 3.3
)
.

The induced inclusion
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\to\PsEq(d^{1},d^{0})
is full and preserves
pullbacks.

Proof.

We recall that a morphism
f\colon(x,\zeta)\to(y,\xi)
of
\PsEq(d^{1},d^{0})
is a morphism
f\colon x\to y
in
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{V})

satisfying
\xi\circ d^{0}f=d^{1}f\circ\zeta
. Thus, when
\zeta

and
\xi
are identities, we precisely obtain a
T
-category functor.

Since
d^{1}
and
d^{0}
are pullback-preserving functors
between categories with pullbacks, it follows that
\PsEq(d^{1},d^{0})
has
pullbacks, and
\PsEq(d^{1},d^{0})\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{V})
creates them. We also note
that
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{V})
preserves pullbacks as well, concluding the
proof.
∎

Lemma 4.8

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Theorem 3.5
)
.

If a morphism
p\colon(x,\mathsf{id})\to(y,\theta)
in
\PsEq(d^{1},d^{0})

is a componentwise epimorphism, then
(y,\theta)\cong(z,\mathsf{id})
for a

T
-category
z
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, we have
d^{0}p=\theta\circ d^{1}p
. Writing
\theta=(\theta_{T},\theta_{d})
, we obtain equations

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=\theta_{T,i}\circ p_{i}

\displaystyle Tp_{1}

\displaystyle=\theta_{d,1}\circ p^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle Tp_{i}

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}_{T,i}\circ p^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle Tp^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=\theta_{d,0}\circ p^{\prime\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle TTp_{i}

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,i}\circ p^{\prime\prime}_{i},

for
i=0,1
from which we deduce

\theta_{T,i}=\mathsf{id},\qquad\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,0}=T\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ\theta_{d,0},\qquad\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}=\theta_{d,1},

since
p
is a componentwise epimorphism.

We claim
(y,\theta)
is isomorphic to a
T
-category. The
construction of the
T
-category presented below is similar to
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma
3.4
. This
T
-category has underlying reflexive
T
-graph

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{0},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}

and we observe that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}}

are pullback diagrams; the left squares are a pullback, and the right
squares commute, and their parallel sides are isomorphisms, as
\theta

is an isomorphism.

Likewise,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTy_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTy_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTy_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTd_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{d,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{d,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}}

is a pullback diagram, since the top left square is a pullback, and the
remaining squares, whose parallel sides are isomorphisms, are commutative,
and therefore pullback diagrams.

Now, we are left with verifying that the relations hold. It is enough to
verify relations of morphisms with (co)domain
Ty_{0},Ty_{1},TTy_{0},TTy_{1}
, as the remaining hold by definition. We have

(\theta^{\prime}_{T,i}\circ d_{1+i})\circ s_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{T,i}\circ e_{i}=e_{x_{i}}\circ\theta_{T,i}=e_{x_{i}},

(\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d_{2})\circ s_{0}=\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ s^{\prime}_{0}\circ d_{1}=Ts_{0}\circ(\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ d_{1}),

Td_{0}\circ Ts_{0}=T(d_{0}\circ s_{0})=\mathsf{id},

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ d_{1}\circ d_{1}

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ m_{0}\circ d^{\prime}_{1}\circ d_{2}

\displaystyle=m_{x_{0}}\circ\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,0}\circ d^{\prime}_{1}\circ d_{2}

\displaystyle=m_{x_{0}}\circ T\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ\theta_{d,0}\circ d^{\prime}_{1}\circ d_{2}

\displaystyle=m_{x_{0}}\circ T\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ Td_{1}\circ\theta_{d,1}\circ d_{2}

\displaystyle=m_{x_{0}}\circ T(\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ d_{1})\circ(\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d_{2}),

\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d_{2}\circ d_{2}=\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ m_{1}\circ d^{\prime}_{2}\circ d_{3}=m_{x_{1}}\circ(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,1}\circ d^{\prime}_{2})\circ d_{3},

\theta^{\prime}_{T,i}\circ d_{1+i}\circ d_{j}=\theta^{\prime}_{T,i}\circ d^{\prime}_{j}\circ d_{2+i}=\begin{cases}(\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d^{\prime}_{1})\circ d_{3}&i=j=1\\
Td_{0}\circ(\theta^{\prime}_{T,i}\circ d_{2+i})&j=0\end{cases}\qquad i,j=0,1,\quad j\leqslant i

T(\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ d_{1})\circ\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d^{\prime}_{0}=T\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ\theta_{d,0}\circ d^{\prime}_{1}\circ d^{\prime}_{0}=\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,0}\circ d^{\prime\prime}_{0}\circ d^{\prime}_{2}=TTd_{0}\circ(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{T,1}\circ d^{\prime}_{2}),

Td_{0}\circ\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d^{\prime}_{1}=\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ d^{\prime}_{0}\circ d^{\prime}_{1}=\theta^{\prime}_{T,0}\circ d^{\prime}_{0}\circ d^{\prime}_{0}=Td_{0}\circ\theta^{\prime}_{T,1}\circ d^{\prime}_{0},

and this concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.9

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma 4.4
)
.

The embedding
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\to\PsEq(d^{1},d^{0})
reflects effective descent
morphisms.

Proof.

Since any effective descent morphism is an epimorphism, this result is an
immediate consequence of Lemma
4.8
and Corollary

2.5
.
∎

Theorem 4.10

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Theorem 4.5
)
.

A functor
p\colon x\to y
of
T
-categories is an effective descent
morphism in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
, provided that

–

Tp_{1}
is an effective descent morphism,

–

Tp_{2}
is a descent morphism, and

–

p_{3}
is an almost descent morphism.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.5
, if
Tp_{2}
is a descent morphism, then so is

p_{2}
, and if
Tp_{1}
is an effective descent morphism, then so are

p_{1}
and
TTp_{1}
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.1
, we may
also deduce that
p_{0},Tp_{0}
and
TTp_{0}
are effective descent
morphisms.

These conditions, and the fact that
p_{3}
is an almost descent morphism,
guarantee that
p
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{V})
, and
the morphism
d^{1}p=d^{0}p
is a descent morphism, as these are
determined componentwise.

Thus, we conclude that
(p,\mathsf{id})
is an effective descent morphism in
\PsEq(d^{1},d^{0})
by Proposition
2.7
, and, by Corollary

4.9
, so is
p
.
∎

4.4
A direct description of effective descent morphisms

We return to the setting of Burroni’s
T
-catégories
, where
\mathcal{V}
is any category with finite limits, and
T
is a monad on
\mathcal{V}
,
not necessarily cartesian. We confirm that the arguments of Le Creurer for
effective descent morphisms of essentially algebraic theories can be applied
just as well to
T
-catégories
.

Throughout, we assume that
p\colon x\to y
is a functor of
T
-categories. We recall that since
p
is a reflexive
T
-graph
morphism, if
\mathcal{E}
is the class of (effective/almost) descent morphisms
in
\mathcal{V}
and
p_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
, then
p_{0}\in\mathcal{E}
as well by
Lemma
4.1
.

Lemma 4.11

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma 5.1
)
.

If
p_{1}
is a (pullback-stable) epimorphism in
\mathcal{V}
, then so is

p
in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
.

Proof.

If
q,r\colon y\to z
are functors such that
q\circ p=r\circ p
, then
q_{i}\circ p_{i}=r_{i}\circ p_{i}
for
i=0,1
, so
q_{i}=r_{i}
, implying
q=r
. We conclude that
p
is an epimorphism.

Since pullbacks in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
are calculated componentwise, our claim
is verified.
∎

Lemma 4.12

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Lemma 5.2
)
.

If
p_{1}
is a (pullback-stable) regular epimorphism, and
p_{2}
is a
(pullback-stable) epimorphism in
\mathcal{V}
, then
p
is a
(pullback-stable) regular epimorphism in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
.

Proof.

We consider the kernel pair of
p
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

(4.8)

If
q\colon x\to z
is a functor such that
r\circ q=s\circ q
, then, when
p_{i}
is a regular epimorphism for
i=0,1
, there
exists a unique
t_{i}\colon y_{i}\to z_{i}
making the triangle of Diagram
(
4.9
) commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}

(4.9)

for
i=0,1
. The morphisms
t_{0},t_{1}
define a functor
t\colon y\to z
of
T
-categories; indeed, we note that, by the
universal property,
t_{2}\circ(g,f)=(t_{1}\circ g,Tt_{1}\circ f)
,
from which we deduce
q_{2}=t_{2}\circ p_{2}
. Then, the following
calculations

\displaystyle t_{1}\circ d_{1}\circ p_{2}

\displaystyle=t_{1}\circ p_{1}\circ d_{1}=q_{1}\circ d_{1}=d_{1}\circ q_{2}=d_{1}\circ t_{2}\circ p_{2},

\displaystyle d_{i}\circ t_{1}\circ p_{1}

\displaystyle=d_{i}\circ q_{1}=T^{i}q_{0}\circ d_{i}=T^{i}t_{0}\circ T^{i}p_{0}\circ d_{i}=T^{i}t_{0}\circ d_{i}\circ p_{1}

plus the fact that
p_{1},p_{2}
are epimorphisms confirm our claim.
Naturally,
t\colon y\to z
is the unique functor making the triangle
below commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

for if
l\colon y\to z
were another such functor, we would deduce
that
l_{i}=t_{i}
by uniqueness given at (
4.9
) for
i=0,1
, so
l=t
.

Again, by componentwise calculation of pullbacks of
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
, we
conclude that if
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are pullback-stable, then so is

p
.
∎

Theorem 4.13

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2023
, Theorem 5.3
)
.

If
T
preserves pullbacks, and

–

p_{1}\colon x_{1}\to y_{1}
is an effective descent morphism,

–

p_{2}\colon x_{2}\to y_{2}
is a descent morphism, and

–

p_{3}\colon x_{3}\to y_{3}
is an almost descent morphism

in
\mathcal{V}
, then
p
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.12
, our hypotheses guarantee that
\mathcal{K}^{p}\colon\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\downarrow y\to\mathsf{Desc}(p)
is fully faithful. Hence, our goal is
to confirm
\mathcal{K}^{p}
is essentially surjective, and we shall do so
via Proposition
2.2
.

We consider the kernel pair (
4.8
) of
p
, and we let
(a\colon v\to x,\gamma\colon r\times_{x}a\to v)
be a discrete fibration
(internal to
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
) over
\Ker(p)
.

If
p_{i}
is an effective descent morphism for
i=0,1
, we obtain an
equivalence
\mathcal{K}^{p_{i}}\colon\mathcal{V}\downarrow y_{i}\to\mathsf{Desc}(p_{i})

for
i=0,1
. Thus, by Proposition
2.2
there
exist
b_{i}\colon w_{i}\to y_{i}
and
h_{i}\colon v_{i}\to w_{i}
such
that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{i}}

(4.10)

is a pullback diagram, satisfying

h_{i}\circ\gamma_{i}=h_{i}\circ\varepsilon_{p_{i}\circ a_{i}}

(4.11)

for
i=0,1
.

We claim that
w
is a
T
-category,
h_{0},h_{1}
define a functor
h\colon v\to w
, and
b_{0},b_{1}
define a functor
b\colon w\to y
. To do so, we consider the kernel pairs of
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
; given
that
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
are descent morphisms,
h_{0}
and
h_{1}

are the coequalizers of their kernel pairs. Moreover, since
T
preserves
kernel pairs, we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{i}u_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{i}v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{i}w_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}

which provides the
T
-graph structure of
w
. With this, we obtain the
following cospan of cospans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tw_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tw_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tb_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tp_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

(4.12)

and since
T
preserves pullbacks, the horizontal and vertical pullbacks
of (
4.12
) are, respectively

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tv_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tv_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Td_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

(4.13)

so, by commutativity of limits, the cospans (
4.13
) have
isomorphic pullbacks. Since the pullback of the last span defines
v_{2}
,
we obtain the following pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

(4.14)

Analogously, we deduce that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{3}}

(4.15)

is a pullback diagram as well.

If
p_{2}
is also a descent morphism, we conclude via (
4.14
)
that
h_{2}
is a regular epimorphism as well. So, we may consider its
kernel pair as well, to obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

which give the identity and composition structure morphisms for
w
.
Indeed, under the hypothesis
w
is a
T
-category, we can already
conclude that
h\colon v\to w
is a functor.

To prove this hypothesis, note that if
p_{3}
is also an almost descent
morphism, then so is
h_{3}
by (
4.15
). Now, we note that we
have

\displaystyle d_{1}\circ s_{0}\circ h_{0}

\displaystyle=Th_{0}\circ d_{1}\circ s_{0}=Th_{0}\circ e=e\circ h_{0}

\displaystyle d_{0}\circ s_{0}\circ h_{0}

\displaystyle=h_{0}\circ d_{0}\circ s_{0}=h_{0}

\displaystyle d_{1}\circ d_{1}\circ h_{2}

\displaystyle=Th_{0}\circ d_{1}\circ d_{1}=Th_{0}\circ m\circ Td_{1}\circ d_{2}=m\circ Td_{1}\circ d_{2}\circ h_{2}

\displaystyle d_{0}\circ d_{1}\circ h_{2}

\displaystyle=h_{0}\circ d_{0}\circ d_{1}=h_{0}\circ d_{0}\circ d_{0}=d_{0}\circ d_{0}\circ h_{2}

\displaystyle d_{1}\circ s_{i}\circ h_{1}

\displaystyle=h_{1}\circ d_{1}\circ s_{i}=h_{1}\circ s_{0}\circ d_{0}=s_{0}\circ d_{0}\circ h_{1}

\displaystyle d_{1}\circ d_{2}\circ h_{3}

\displaystyle=h_{1}\circ d_{1}\circ d_{2}=h_{1}\circ d_{1}\circ d_{1}=d_{1}\circ d_{1}\circ h_{3}

so, by cancellation, we conclude that
w
is a
T
-category.

Finally, we must confirm
b_{0},b_{1}
define a functor
b\colon w\to y
. We recall that
h_{0},h_{1},h_{2}
are epimorphisms, and we observe that

\displaystyle b_{1}\circ d_{1}\circ h_{2}

\displaystyle=b_{1}\circ h_{1}\circ d_{1}=p_{1}\circ a_{1}\circ d_{1}=d_{1}\circ p_{2}\circ a_{2}=d_{1}\circ b_{2}\circ h_{2}

\displaystyle d_{i}\circ b_{1}\circ h_{1}

\displaystyle=d_{i}\circ p_{1}\circ a_{1}=T^{i}p_{0}\circ T^{i}a_{0}\circ d_{i}=T^{i}b_{0}\circ T^{i}h_{0}\circ d_{i}=T^{i}b_{0}\circ d_{i}\circ h_{1},

confirming that the properties for a functor are satisfied, via cancellation.

Since (
4.10
) is a pullback diagram for
i=0,1
, we obtain a
pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

and we have

h\circ\gamma=h\circ\varepsilon_{p\circ a}

(4.16)

as a consequence of (
4.11
) for
i=0,1
. This concludes
our proof, by Proposition
2.2
.
∎

4.5
Application to graded, operadic and enhanced multicategories

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a category with finite limits, and
T
a cartesian monad
on
\mathcal{V}
. We recall that we have an equivalence

\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\downarrow x\simeq\mathsf{Cat}(T_{x},\mathcal{V}\downarrow x_{0})

(4.17)

for any internal
(T,\mathcal{V})
-category
x
, where
T_{x}
is a
cartesian monad on
\mathcal{V}\downarrow x_{0}
induced by
x
; see
Leinster, 2004
, Section
6.2
. Since
\mathcal{C}\downarrow x\to\mathcal{C}
creates (effective,
almost) descent morphisms, we can obtain results about effective descent
morphisms in
\mathsf{Cat}(T_{x},\mathcal{V}\downarrow x_{0})
by studying those of
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
.

To illustrate this, let
\mathcal{V}
be a category with finite limits. For
an internal category
\mathcal{C}
in
\mathcal{V}
, the monad on
\mathcal{V}\downarrow\mathcal{C}_{0}
induced by the identity monad on
\mathcal{V}
is denoted
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}-
. Via the equivalence (
4.17
), the
category of
\mathcal{C}
-graded categories internal to
\mathcal{V}
is the
category of internal functors over
\mathcal{C}
:

\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}-,\mathcal{V}\downarrow\mathcal{C}_{0})\simeq\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})\downarrow\mathcal{C}.

Hence, the study of effective descent functors of graded internal categories
reduces to the study of effective descent functor of internal categories. Of
particular importance is the case
\mathcal{C}_{0}\cong 1
; that is, when
\mathcal{C}
is an internal
\mathcal{V}
-monoid.

Now, let
\mathcal{V}
be a lextensive category. The free monoid monad
\mathfrak{M}
on
\mathcal{V}
, given on objects by

X\mapsto\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X^{n},

is a cartesian monad, hence we may consider the category of

multicategories
internal to
\mathcal{V}
, given by
\mathsf{MultiCat}(\mathcal{V})=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{V})
. By Theorem
4.13
, a multicategory
functor
p\colon x\to y
internal to
\mathcal{V}
is an effective
descent morphism in
\mathsf{MultiCat}(\mathcal{V})
, provided that

–

p_{1}\colon x_{1}\to y_{1}
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}
,

–

p_{2}\colon x_{2}\to y_{2}
is a descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}
,

–

p_{3}\colon x_{3}\to y_{3}
is an almost descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}
.

If we have an internal
\mathcal{V}
-operad
\mathfrak{O}
(an internal
\mathcal{V}
-multicategory with terminal object-of-objects), the monad
T_{\mathfrak{O}}
induced by the free monoid monad is given by

X\mapsto\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{O}_{n}\times X^{n},

and is cartesian as well. We define the objects of
\mathsf{Cat}(T_{\mathfrak{O}},\mathcal{V})
to be the
operadic
multicategories internal to
\mathcal{V}
. Via (
4.17
), we obtain

\mathsf{Cat}(T_{\mathfrak{O}},\mathcal{V})\simeq\mathsf{MultiCat}(\mathcal{V})\downarrow\mathfrak{O},

so a morphism of operadic multicategories is effective for descent if it is so
as a morphism on the underlying internal multicategories, which we have
described above.

Finally, we consider the 2-monad
\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}
on
\mathsf{Cat}
, the

free finite coproduct completion
2-monad. This is one of the central
objects of study in
Weber, 2007
, where it was shown to be a cartesian
(2-)monad. Hence, we may consider
\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}
-categories internal to

\mathsf{Cat}
, and we can obtain a description of the effective descent morphisms
of
\mathsf{Cat}(\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}},\mathsf{Cat})
via Theorem
4.13
.

Moreover, we have a cartesian 2-natural transformation
\mathfrak{S}\to\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}
(see
Weber, 2007
, Example 7.5
), where
\mathfrak{S}
is the
free symmetric strict monoidal category 2-monad, and the objects of
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathfrak{S},\mathsf{Cat})
were called
enhanced symmetric
multicategories
in
Leinster, 2004
, p. 212
, whose study of effective descent
functors reduces to the previous case.

By analogy, we may take the objects of
\mathsf{Cat}(\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}},\mathsf{Cat})
to be the
enhanced
cocartesian
multicategories, and analogously, we have the
free finite product completion 2-monad
\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}^{*}
on
\mathsf{Cat}
,
defined on objects by
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}
, which is
also cartesian, and the objects of
\mathsf{Cat}(\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}^{*},\mathsf{Cat})
may be called
enhanced
cartesian
multicategories
2
2

2

These are related to the

multi-sorted Lawvere theories
– see
Cruttwell & Shulman, 2010
, or Subsection 5.4.2
.

For an ordinary functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
of categories, we
have that

–

F
is an almost descent functor if
F
is surjective on morphisms,

–

F
is a descent functor if
F
is surjective on 2-chains,

–

F
is an effective descent functor if
F
is surjective on 3-chains,

so, for
T
a cartesian monad on
\mathsf{Cat}
(such as one of
\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}},\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}^{*},\mathfrak{S}
), a functor
P\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
of
T
-categories is effective for descent if

–

P_{1}\colon\mathcal{X}_{1}\to\mathcal{Y}_{1}
is surjective on 3-chains,

–

P_{2}\colon\mathcal{X}_{2}\to\mathcal{Y}_{2}
is surjective on 2-chains,

–

P_{3}\colon\mathcal{X}_{3}\to\mathcal{Y}_{3}
is surjective on morphisms.

Chapter 5
Generalized enriched multicategory functors

Equipped with the description of effective descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})

given in Theorem
4.13
, our goal is to study effective
descent morphisms in a category of enriched generalized multicategories, by
suitably embedding it into a category of internal generalized multicategories,
and studying whether the effective descent morphisms are reflected by this
embedding. In short, we aim to generalize the approach of
Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem
9.11
for the embedding
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
to the setting of
generalized multicategorical structures.

Such an embedding is constructed via a suitable notion of

change-of-base
for generalized multicategories. This work was carried
out in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
from a general point-of-view; here, we recount the details
that are relevant in the study of effective descent functors for enriched
generalized multicategories.

The notion of enriched
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories was introduced in

Clementino & Tholen, 2003
, under the terminology
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories, as a suitable
notion of
lax algebras
. In Section
5.1
, we will provide
the definition in a slightly more general setting, as done in
Seal, 2005
and

Hofmann et al., 2014
(when
\mathcal{V}
is a suitable quantale), as well
as
Cruttwell & Shulman, 2010
. We consider a monad
T=(T,m,e)
on
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
in
\mathsf{Equip}_{\mathsf{lax}}
– the 2-category of
equipments
,
lax functors

and
icons
, and the enriched
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories are defined
as a suitable notion of lax algebra. The original setting of
Clementino & Tholen, 2003
is
recovered when
T
is a
normal
lax monad.

Working in the 2-category
\mathsf{Equip}_{\mathsf{lax}}
, in Section

5.2
, we review the lifting of the functor
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
to a functor
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\mathsf{Span}(\mathcal{V})

in Proposition
5.1
, and we describe the monad
\overline{T}
on
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
, given by reflecting the monad
T
on
\mathsf{Span}(\mathcal{V})
along
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
. The results of
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
on change-of-base for generalized categorical
structures then confirm that we obtain an embedding

-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V}),

(5.1)

under a suitable condition (Theorem
5.2
).

In Section
5.3
, we study the problem of reflecting
effective descent morphisms along the embedding (
5.1
). We
confirm that, under a second suitable condition, every effective descent
morphism is reflected (Lemma
5.5
). Then, via our
description of the effective descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
, we obtain
Theorem
5.6
, the main result of this chapter.

We finish this chapter with Section
5.4
, where we provide our
applications in the study of effective descent morphisms in categorical
structures, after briefly discussing whether the two extra conditions
introduced in Sections
5.2
and
5.3

affect the scope of the available examples.

5.1
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories

Throughout this chapter,
\mathcal{V}
is assumed to be a lextensive, cartesian
monoidal category. We recall that
\mathcal{V}
is necessarily distributive;
see
Carboni et al., 1993
, Proposition 4.5
.

The equipment of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices, denoted
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
, is defined in

Betti et al., 1983
; we simply recall that a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
r
is a family

(r(x,y))_{x,y\in X\times Y}
of objects in
\mathcal{V}
indexed by
X\times Y
. Such a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix shall be denoted as
r\colon X\to Y
. If
s\colon Y\to Z
is another
\mathcal{V}
-matrix, the composite

s\cdot r\colon X\to Z
is given at
x,z
by

(s\cdot r)(x,z)=\sum_{y\in Y}s(y,z)\times r(x,y),

and for every set
X
, the unit
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
1_{X}\colon X\to X

is given at
x,y
by the terminal object if
x=y
, and the initial
object if
x\neq y
.

We let
\mathsf{Equip}_{\mathsf{lax}}
be the 2-category of
equipments
,
lax
functors
, and
icons

Lack, 2010
, and we let
T=(T,m,e)
be a
monad on
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
in the 2-category
\mathsf{Equip}_{\mathsf{lax}}
. We remark that
T
has an underlying monad on
\mathsf{Set}
, and it can be shown that
T
is
its
lax extension
, under the terminology of
Clementino & Tholen, 2003
, p. 18
,
provided we relax the condition
T1_{r}=1_{Tr}
for
\mathcal{V}
-matrices

r
. This was observed in
Hofmann et al., 2014
, Subsection 1.13
when
\mathcal{V}

is a quantale, and in
Cruttwell & Shulman, 2010
, Appendix B
.

The data for such a lax monad
T
consists of

–

a set
TX
for each set
X
,

–

a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
Tr\colon TX\to TY
for each
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
r\colon X\to Y
,

–

for each set
X
, a family of comparison morphisms

\unit^{T}_{X}\colon 1\to(T1_{X})(\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{x})

indexed by
\mathfrak{x}\in TX
,

–

for each pair of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices
r\colon X\to Y
,
s\colon Y\to Z
, a family of comparison morphisms

\mult^{T}_{r,s}\colon(Ts\cdot Tr)(\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{z})\to(T(s\cdot r))(\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{z})

indexed by
\mathfrak{x}\in TX
,
\mathfrak{z}\in TZ
,

–

for each
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
r\colon X\to Y
, a family of morphisms

e_{r,x,y}\colon r(x,y)\to(Tr)(e(x),e(y))

indexed by
x\in X
,
y\in Y
,

–

for each
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
r\colon X\to Y
, a family of morphisms

m_{r,\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{y}}\colon(TTr)(\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{y})\to(Tr)(m(\mathfrak{x}),m(\mathfrak{y}))

indexed by
\mathfrak{x}\in TTX
,
\mathfrak{y}\in TTY
.

satisfying the following coherence conditions, where we omit the indexing
elements, as well as the associator and unitor isomorphisms for convenience:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tt\cdot Ts\cdot Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(t\cdot s)\cdot Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tt\cdot T(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(t\cdot s\cdot r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{T}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr\cdot T1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T1\cdot Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{T}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTs\cdot TTr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ts\cdot Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(Ts\cdot Tr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT(s\cdot r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{s}\cdot m_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mult^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{s\cdot r}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{TTx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T1_{Tx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT1_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Tx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T1_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{m_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\unit^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\cdot r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ts\cdot Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(s\cdot r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{s}\cdot e_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{s\cdot r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Tx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T1_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{e_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{T}}

for each 3-chain of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices
r,\,s,\,t
, as well as the
following associativity and identity conditions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Te_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{r}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{r}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTTr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tm_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{r}}

where we have also omitted the indexing elements.

An enriched
(T,\mathcal{V})
-category is a quadruple
(X,a,\upsilon,\mu)
,
where
X
is a set,
a\colon TX\to X
is a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix,
\upsilon
is a family of morphisms
\upsilon_{x}\colon 1\to a(e(x),x)

indexed by
x\in X
, and
\mu
is a family of morphisms

\mu_{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}}\colon a(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})\to a(m(x_{2}),x_{0})

indexed by
x_{i}\in T^{i}X
, where we define

a(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})=a(x_{1},x_{0})\times(Ta)(x_{2},x_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad a(x_{3},x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})=a(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})\times(TTa)(x_{3},x_{2})

for
x_{i}\in T^{i}X
. These families satisfy the following identity
and associativity laws:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(x_{1},x_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(e_{T}(x_{1}),x_{1},x_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(x_{1},x_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times((T\upsilon)_{x_{1}}\circ\unit^{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(x_{1},x_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(e_{T}(x_{1}),e(x_{0}),x_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(x_{1},x_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon_{x_{0}}\times e_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(x_{3},x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a((Tm)(x_{3}),x_{1},x_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a((mT)(x_{3}),m(x_{2}),x_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a((m\circ mT)(x_{3}),x_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times(T\mu\circ\mult^{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\times m_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

5.2
Change-of-base and embedding

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a lextensive category. The following diagram depicts
the adjunction fundamental to our study of viewing enriched generalized
multicategories as internal generalized multicategories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1{,}-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(5.2)

We shall denote the counit of this adjunction by
\hat{\varepsilon}
. As was done in

Lucatelli, 2018a
, Theorem 9.11
, we will assume for the remainder of this chapter
that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
is fully faithful, so that
\mathsf{Set}
may be the seen as
the full subcategory of
\mathcal{V}
consisting of the
discrete
objects
. As observed in
Verity, 1992
, Lemma 2.2.1
(when
\mathcal{V}
is a
presheaf category), we have:

Proposition 5.1

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Remark 7.4, Lemma 7.6
)
.

We have an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Span}(\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1{,}-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(5.3)

in the 2-category
\mathsf{Equip}_{\mathsf{lax}}
. Moreover,
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\mathsf{Span}(\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful, and the underlying adjunction on the categories
of objects is precisely (
5.2
).

Spans
a\colon X\to Y
in a category
\mathcal{V}
are denoted by a
diagram such as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{a}}

So, if
a\colon X\to Y
is a span, the
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
\mathcal{V}(1,a)
is given at
x\in\mathcal{V}(1,X)
,
y\in\mathcal{V}(1,Y)
by the
pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,a)(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x,y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{a},r_{a}}

while if
r\colon S\to T
is a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix, the span
r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
is given by the coproduct of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

indexed by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

Now, we let
T=(T,m,e)
be a cartesian monad on
\mathcal{V}
. By

Hermida, 2000
, Proposition A.2
,
T
induces a pseudomonad on
\mathsf{Span}(\mathcal{V})
,
which may be seen as a monad in
\mathsf{Equip}_{\mathsf{lax}}
, which we also denote by
T
; see
Cruttwell & Shulman, 2010
, Example A.6
.

Via (
5.3
), the monad
T
on
\mathsf{Span}(\mathcal{V})

induces a monad
\overline{T}=(\overline{T},\overline{m},\overline{e})
on
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}

(see
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Proposition 8.1
), where

–

\overline{T}a=\mathcal{V}(1,T(a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1))
,

–

\overline{m}_{a}=\mathcal{V}(1,m_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ T\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)})

–

\overline{e}_{a}=\mathcal{V}(1,e_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1})\circ\hat{\eta}_{a}
,

for each
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
a
. This monad is the lax extension of the
monad (also denoted
\overline{T}
) induced by
T
on
\mathsf{Set}
via (
5.2
). Under suitable conditions, the category of
enriched
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})
-categories is embedded in the category of internal

(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories, which are our objects of interest in this
Chapter.

Theorem 5.2

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Lemma 9.1, Theorem 9.2
)
.

If
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
is a cartesian natural transformation,
we have an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1{,}-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(5.4)

and the functor
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful.

The embedding functor
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
is given on an
enriched
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})
-category
(X,a,\upsilon,\mu)
by the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}}

with unit
\hat{\upsilon}
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(e(x),x)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\eta}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon_{x}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{e(x),x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\upsilon}}

via the universal property of the coproduct, and the composition
\hat{\mu}
is
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(m(x_{2}),x_{0})\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}\in T^{i}X}a(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{x_{2},x_{1},x_{0}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mu}}

via the universal property of the coproduct. The hypothesis that
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
is a cartesian natural transformation ensures that the
following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}\in T^{i}X}\overline{T}a(x_{2},x_{1})\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tr_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}

is a pullback square, by
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Lemma 8.3
, thereby guaranteeing that

\sum_{x_{i}\in T^{i}X}a(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})

is the object of 2-chains of
(X,a,\upsilon,\mu)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
.

If
(f,\phi)\colon(X,a,\upsilon,\mu)\to(Y,b,\upsilon,\mu)
is an
enriched
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})
-functor,
(f,\phi)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
is given on objects
by
f\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
, and
\phi\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon M_{a\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\to M_{b\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
on morphisms.

5.3
Reflection of effective descent morphisms

Having established all the necessary notation, we can now proceed to study the
effective descent morphisms in
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
, by studying whether the
embedding
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
reflects effective descent
morphisms. The key idea, developed by the next result, is that we must
guarantee that the full inclusion
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
consists precisely
of those internal
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories with a discrete object of
objects, which could not be guaranteed in general.

Lemma 5.3

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Lemma 10.2
)
.

Let
X
be an internal
(T,\mathcal{V})
-category whose object-of-objects
is discrete; that is,
X_{0}\cong S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
for a set
S
.

If we let
a
be the span in
\mathcal{V}
given by the underlying

T
-graph of
X
, as depicted in (
5.5
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

(5.5)

then
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}
is a split epimorphism. Moreover, if
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}\colon\overline{T}1\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to T1
is a monomorphism, then
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}
is an
isomorphism.

Proof.

We may assume that
X_{0}=S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
. Our first step is to notice that
d_{1}\colon X_{1}\to T(S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)
factors uniquely through
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
; we have
e_{1}=\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}\circ(\overline{e}_{1}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)
(by
definition of
\overline{e}
), so there exists a unique
\hat{d}_{1}
,
depicted by dashed morphism in (
5.6
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}1\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{d}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{e}_{1}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}!\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(!\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}}

(5.6)

making the adjacent diagrams commute.

We may conclude that there is a unique morphism
\omega\colon X_{1}\to M_{\mathcal{V}(1,a)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
such that
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}\circ\omega=\mathsf{id}

(confirming that
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}
is a split epimorphism) and
(\hat{d}_{1},d_{0})=(d_{1},d_{0})\circ\omega
, as depicted in
(
5.7
)
1
1

1

It should be noted that
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}
is

defined
by this pullback square, see
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, (2.5)
, noting
that
\mathcal{V}
is lextensive.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\mathcal{V}(1,a)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\times S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\times S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{d}_{1},d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathcal{V}(1,a)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1},r_{\mathcal{V}(1,a)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1},d_{0}}

(5.7)

Moreover, if
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}
is a monomorphism, then, by the pullback
square in (
5.6
), so is
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
, and by the
pullback square in (
5.7
), we conclude
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}
is a
monomorphism. Therefore,
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}
must be invertible.
∎

As a corollary, we conclude that the enriched
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})
-categories
are precisely the internal
T
-categories whose object of objects
is discrete. More precisely, we have:

Lemma 5.4

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Theorem 10.3
)
.

If
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}
is a monomorphism, we have a pseudopullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

of categories with pullbacks and pullback-preserving functors.

Proof.

The objects of the pseudopullback are triples
(S,X,\phi)
where
S

is a set,
X
is an internal
(T,\mathcal{V})
-category, and
\phi
is an isomorphism
\phi\colon S\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to X
. By Lemma

5.3
, it follows that
\hat{\varepsilon}_{a}
is invertible,
where
a
is the span given by the underlying
T
-graph of
X
, as
in (
5.5
).

By general remarks about change-of-base adjunctions between horizontal lax
algebras given in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Section 6
, this implies that
X
is
isomorphic to an enriched
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})
-category.
∎

Everything is set up to apply the results about effective descent morphisms in
bilimits from Chapter
2
, which gives the following reflection
result:

Lemma 5.5

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Lemma 10.4
)
.

If
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}
is a monomorphism, then
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
reflects effective descent morphisms.

Proof.

We follow the same approach as Theorem
3.9
. As
stated in Remark
2.9
, the functor
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}

preserves descent morphisms, which are reflected by
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
.

Since descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Set}
are effective for descent, we may
apply Proposition
2.6
and Lemma
5.4
to
conclude our result.
∎

Now, we can apply our knowledge of effective descent morphisms in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})

to obtain the main result of this chapter:

Theorem 5.6

(
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Theorem 10.5
)
.

Let
(f,\phi)\colon(X,a,\upsilon,\mu)\to(Y,b,\upsilon,\mu)
be a
functor of enriched
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})
-categories. If
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}

is a monomorphism, and if

–

((f,\phi)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)_{1}
is an effective descent morphism,

–

((f,\phi)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)_{2}
is a descent morphism,

–

((f,\phi)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)_{3}
is an almost descent morphism,

then
(f,\phi)
is an effective descent morphism in
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.13
, the above conditions guarantee that
(f,\phi)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})
. Since

\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}
is a monomorphism, we may apply Lemma

5.5
to conclude that
(f,\phi)
is an
effective descent morphism in
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
.
∎

5.4
Scope of the findings

Our main result holds in the context of a lextensive category
\mathcal{V}

such that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
is fully faithful. These
properties are enjoyed by the categories
\mathsf{Cat}
,
\mathsf{Top}
, any connected
Grothendieck topos, and any free coproduct completion
\Fam(\mathcal{B})
of a
category
\mathcal{B}
with finite limits. Moreover, we have required two more
hypotheses:

(a)

\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
is a cartesian natural transformation.

(b)

\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}
is a monomorphism.

We can verify that
(b)
holds when

•

the terminal object is a
separator
, that is, when
\mathcal{V}(1,-)
is faithful, so that
\hat{\varepsilon}
is a componentwise monomorphism. This
is the case for
\mathsf{Cat}
,
\mathsf{Top}
, any
hyperconnected

Grothendieck topos (by definition), but not the case for
\mathsf{Grph}
nor

\Fam(\mathsf{Set})
2
2

2

These are Grothendieck toposes which are not
hyperconnected.
,

•

T
is
discrete
, that is
\hat{\varepsilon}_{T1}
is an isomorphism.
This is the case when
T
is the free monoid monad on any category
\mathcal{V}
under the above conditions, but not when
T
is the free
category monad on
\mathsf{Grph}
.

Further discussion may be found in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Section 10
.

On the other hand, we have found no examples of cartesian monads
T
on
\mathcal{V}
that do not satisfy
(a)
. This is discussed at
length in
Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Section 8
; here we merely recall the results required
to discuss our examples.

5.4.1
Classical multicategories

The free monoid monad
\mathfrak{M}
on
\mathcal{V}
satisfies

(a)
and
(b)

Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Lemma 8.7 and
Subsection 10.2
. In this case, we let
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{MultiCat}=(\overline{\mathfrak{M}},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
be the category of

enriched
\mathcal{V}
-multicategories
.

If
(f,\phi)\colon(X,a,\upsilon,\mu)\to(Y,b,\upsilon,\mu)
is a
functor of enriched
\mathcal{V}
-multicategories such that

\phi\colon\sum_{x_{i}\in(\mathfrak{M}^{i}f)^{*}(y_{i})}a(x_{1},x_{0})\to b(y_{1},y_{0})

is an effective descent morphism,

\phi\times\mathfrak{M}\phi\colon\sum_{x_{i}\in(\mathfrak{M}^{i}f)^{*}(y_{i})}a(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})\to b(y_{2},y_{1},y_{0})

is a descent morphism, and

\phi\times\mathfrak{M}\phi\times\mathfrak{M}^{2}\phi\colon\sum_{x_{i}\in(\mathfrak{M}^{i}f)^{*}(y_{i})}a(x_{3},x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})\to b(y_{3},y_{2},y_{1},y_{0})

is an almost descent morphism, for all
y_{i}\in\mathfrak{M}^{i}Y
with
i=0,1,2,3
, then
(f,\phi)
is an effective descent morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{MultiCat}
, by Theorem
5.6
.

5.4.2
Cartesian and cocartesian multicategories

The free finite coproduct completion
\Fam_{\mathsf{fin}}
on
\mathsf{Cat}
satisfies

(a)

Prezado & Lucatelli, 2024
, Subsection 8.5
. Since the terminal
category is a separator in
\mathsf{Cat}
, we conclude that

(b)
holds. Thus, by Lemma
5.4
,
(\overline{\Fam}_{\mathsf{fin}},\mathsf{Cat})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
is the category of enhanced
cocartesian multicategories with a discrete object-of-objects. For this
reason, we refer to its objects as cocartesian multicategories. Likewise,
(\overline{\Fam}^{*}_{\mathsf{fin}},\mathsf{Cat})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
is the category of cartesian
multicategories
3
3

3

These are a
wide
subcategory of the category
of multi-sorted Lawvere theories – the morphisms between cartesian
multicategories are precisely the “degree one” morphisms between Lawvere
theories. See also
Cruttwell & Shulman, 2010
, Example 4.17
.
.

Via the description of effective descent morphisms for functors of enhanced
multicategories, given in Section
4.5
we obtain a
description of the effective descent functors for cartesian and cocartesian
multicategories.

5.4.3
Graded, operadic and symmetric multicategories

Let
S,T
be endofunctors on
\mathcal{V}
, and let
\alpha\colon S\to T
be a cartesian natural transformation. If
T
satisfies

(a)
, so does
S
. Thus, it follows that

(a)
is satisfied by
\mathcal{V}
-operadic monads, as well
as the free symmetric strict monoidal category monad
\mathfrak{S}
, when

\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Cat}
.

However, we do not guarantee that every
\mathcal{V}
-operadic monad satisfies

(b)
in general. It certainly is true if the terminal
object of
\mathcal{V}
is a separator, and if
\mathfrak{O}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-operad such that
\mathfrak{O}_{n}
is discrete for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
then the
\mathcal{V}
-operadic monad
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathfrak{O}}
induced
by
\mathfrak{O}
is discrete, so the property is also satisfied in this
setting. We call such
\mathcal{V}
-operads
discrete
.

If the
\mathcal{V}
-operad
\mathfrak{O}
is discrete, we let
(\overline{\mathfrak{M}}_{\mathfrak{O}},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
be the category of

enriched
\mathfrak{O}
-categories
. Via the results of

4.5
, and Theorem
5.6
, we obtain a
description for the effective descent functors of enriched
\mathfrak{O}
-categories. In particular, we also obtain the
enriched graded
multicategories
by a discrete
\mathcal{V}
-monoid
M
, and a description of
the respective effective descent functors.

Since the terminal category is a separator in
\mathsf{Cat}
, it follows that

(b)
is satisfied for
\mathfrak{S}
. Arguing as we did
in the case of (co)cartesian multicategories, we let
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\mathsf{Cat})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
be the category of
symmetric multicategories
,
for which we also obtain a description of the respective effective descent
functors.

Chapter 6
Fibration of split opfibrations

The bifibration
F_{D}=\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Set})\colon\mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
of discrete
opfibrations is the main object of study in
Sobral, 2004
. Therein, the
functors
p\colon E\to B
of (effective)
F_{D}
-descent were
characterized. Indeed,
p
is a
F_{D}
-descent morphism if and only if

p
is a
lax epimorphism
, while
p
is an effective
F_{D}
-descent morphism if and only if
p
is a
fully faithful
lax
epimorphism.

Our goal for this chapter, covering the work done in
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, is to show
that the results of
Sobral, 2004
for discrete opfibrations can be applied
just as well to other settings. Among them, we are able to characterize the
effective
F
-descent morphisms for the bifibration
F=\mathsf{CAT}(-,\mathsf{Cat})\colon\mathsf{Cat}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
of split opfibrations. We confirm that a functor

p
is of (effective)
F
-descent if and only if it is of (effective)

F_{D}
-descent (Theorem
6.11
).

We begin Section
6.1
by recalling the notions of fully faithful
morphism and lax epimorphism in a 2-category
\mathbb{A}
, as well as a
couple of relevant results. Then we restrict our attention to the setting of
enriched categories, recalling from
Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
the notions of
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful and
\mathcal{V}
-lax epimorphic
\mathcal{V}
-functors, for
\mathcal{V}
a complete and cocomplete symmetric monoidal closed category, and
comparing them with the notions of fully faithful morphism and lax epimorphism
in the 2-category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
of
small

\mathcal{V}
-categories.

6.1
Fully faithful morphisms and lax epimorphisms

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a 2-category. A morphism
f\colon x\to y
is said
to be

–

fully faithful
if
\mathbb{A}(w,f)\colon\mathbb{A}(w,x)\to\mathbb{A}(w,y)
is fully faithful for all
w
,

–

lax epimorphic
if
\mathbb{A}(f,z)\colon\mathbb{A}(y,z)\to\mathbb{A}(x,z)
is fully faithful for all
z
.

A comprehensive study of lax epimorphisms in a 2-category is carried out in

Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
. We shall recall some fundamental aspects.

If we have an adjunction
f\dashv g
in
\mathbb{A}
, the following are
equivalent:

–

the unit of
f\dashv g
is invertible,

–

g
is fully faithful,

–

f
is a lax epimorphism.

Codually, it follows that the following are equivalent:

–

the counit of
f\dashv g
is invertible,

–

g
is a lax epimorphism,

–

f
is fully faithful.

From this, we may conclude that:

Proposition 6.1

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, p. 134
)
.

If a morphism
f
has a left or right adjoint, and is a fully faithful
lax epimorphism, then
f
is an equivalence.

For the remainder of this chapter, we assume that
\mathcal{V}
is a complete
and cocomplete, symmetric monoidal closed category. We now restrict our scope
to the 2-category
\mathbb{A}=\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
of small
\mathcal{V}
-categories,
\mathcal{V}
-functors and
\mathcal{V}
-natural transformations; this setting was
studied in
Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
, Section 5
. We will recall the pertinent definitions
and results therein.

A
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon E\to B
between small
\mathcal{V}
-categories is said to be

–

\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful if
p\colon E(a,b)\to B(pa,pb)
is an
isomorphism for all objects
a,b
,

–

a
\mathcal{V}
-lax epimorphism if the
\mathcal{V}
-functor

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}[p,C]\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}[B,C]\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}[E,C]

is
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful for all small
\mathcal{V}
-categories

C

Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
, Definition 5.4
.

Proposition 6.2

(
Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
, Lemma 5.1
)
.

If
p\colon E\to B
is a
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful
\mathcal{V}
-functor, then
p
is a fully faithful morphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
. The
converse holds if
p
has a left or right adjoint.

Proposition 6.3

(
Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
, Theorem 5.6
)
.

Let
p\colon E\to B
be a
\mathcal{V}
-functor. The following are
equivalent:

–

p
is a lax epimorphism in
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
,

–

p
is a
\mathcal{V}
-lax epimorphism,

–

the functor
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(B,\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(E,\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful.

The result analogous to Proposition
6.3
for
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful
\mathcal{V}
-functors, despite not being a consequence of
duality, can be shown to hold as well:

Lemma 6.4

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Proposition 2.4
)
.

A
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon E\to B
is
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful
if and only if the functor
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
is a lax epimorphism.

Proof.

We have an adjunction
\Lan_{p}\dashv\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
. From previous
remarks,
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
is a lax epimorphism if and only if
\Lan_{p}
is fully faithful.

By the enriched Yoneda lemma,
p
is
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful if and
only if
\Lan_{p}
is fully faithful (see
Kelly, 2005
, Proposition
4.23
).
∎

6.2
Enriched Cauchy completion

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (possibly large)
\mathcal{V}
-category. An object
x
on
\mathcal{C}
is said to be
tiny
(also called
absolutely
presentable
in
Borceux & Dejean, 1986
and
small-projective
in
Kelly, 2005
) if
the representable
\mathcal{V}
-functor
\mathcal{C}(x,-)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{V}
preserves colimits.

The
Cauchy completion
of a small
\mathcal{V}
-category
X
is the
full sub-
\mathcal{V}
-category of tiny objects of
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[X^{\mathsf{op}},\mathcal{V}]
,
and is denoted by
\Cauchy X
. We observe that, in general,
\Cauchy X
is
not a small
\mathcal{V}
-category; for instance, let
\mathcal{V}
be the
category of complete lattices. In
Kelly, 2005
, Section 5.5
, it was shown that

\Cauchy I
is not small, where
I
is the unit
\mathcal{V}
-category. Hence, we will assume for the remainder of this chapter that:

\Cauchy X
is a small
\mathcal{V}
-category for all small
\mathcal{V}
-categories
X
.

(6.1)

This property holds for many base categories
\mathcal{V}
of our interest,
such as
\mathsf{Cat}
,
\mathsf{Set}
, and any small quantale. More generally, it was
shown in
Johnson, 1989
that if the underlying category of
\mathcal{V}
is
locally presentable, then (
6.1
) holds.

The following result confirms that
\mathcal{V}
-equivalences preserve tiny
objects:

Lemma 6.5

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Lemma 2.1
)
.

Let
F\dashv G\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-adjunction of
(possibly large)
\mathcal{V}
-categories. If
G
preserves colimits, then

F
preserves tiny objects.

Proof.

If
a
is tiny, then
\mathcal{D}(Fa,-)\cong\mathcal{C}(a,G(-))
is a
composite of functors that preserve colimits, hence
Fa
is tiny.
∎

A consequence of Lemma
6.5
is that for any
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon X\to Y
, we may define
\Cauchy p\colon\Cauchy X\to\Cauchy Y
by restricting the enriched left Kan extension
\Lan_{p}\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[X,\mathcal{V}]\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[Y,\mathcal{V}]
to the tiny objects. Indeed, we
have a chain of
\mathcal{V}
-adjunctions

\Lan_{p}\dashv\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[p,\mathcal{V}]\dashv\Ran_{p}

which confirms that
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[p,\mathcal{V}]
preserves colimits, so
\Lan_{p}

preserves tiny objects.

By the enriched Yoneda lemma, we readily confirm that any representable

\mathcal{V}
-presheaf is tiny,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[X^{\mathsf{op}},\mathcal{V}](X(-,x),\colim(W,F))

\displaystyle\cong\colim(W,F)x

\displaystyle\cong\colim(W,F(-,x))

\displaystyle\cong\colim(W,\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[X^{\mathsf{op}},\mathcal{V}](X(-,x),F)),

so that the Yoneda
\mathcal{V}
-embedding
\mathfrak{y}\colon X\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}[X^{\mathsf{op}},\mathcal{V}]
restricts to a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
\eta_{X}\colon X\to\Cauchy X
. It also follows that for any
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon X\to Y
, we have a
\mathcal{V}
-natural isomorphism
\eta\circ p\cong\Cauchy p\circ\eta
, and that
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\eta,\mathcal{V})
is an
equivalence.

This allows us to highlight the relationship between Cauchy completion and
fully faithful morphisms/lax epimorphisms:

Lemma 6.6

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Lemma 2.2
)
.

If
p\colon X\to Y
is a
\mathcal{V}
-functor, then the induced
functor

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(Y,\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(X,\mathcal{V})

is fully faithful (respectively, a lax epimorphism) if and only if
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\Cauchy p,\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful (respectively, a lax
epimorphism).

Proof.

We observe that the following diagram commutes up to isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\Cauchy Y,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(Y,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\Cauchy X,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(X,\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\eta_{Y},\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\Cauchy p,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\eta_{X},\mathcal{V})}

Since the rows are equivalences, the result follows.
∎

We immediately conclude that:

Corollary 6.7

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Proposition 2.3
)
.

The following are equivalent for a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon X\to Y
:

(i)

p
is a lax epimorphism,

(ii)

\Cauchy p
is a lax epimorphism,

(iii)

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful.

(iv)

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\Cauchy p,\mathcal{V})
is fully faithful.

Proof.

The equivalences
(i)

\iff

(iii)
and

(ii)

\iff

(iv)
were obtained in
Proposition
6.3
, and the equivalence
(iii)

\iff

(iv)
follows by Lemma
6.6
.
∎

Corollary 6.8

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Proposition 2.4
)
.

The following are equivalent for a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon X\to Y
:

(i)

p
is
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful,

(ii)

\Cauchy p
is
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful,

(iii)

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
is a lax epimorphism.

(iv)

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(\Cauchy p,\mathcal{V})
is a lax epimorphism.

Proof.

The equivalences
(i)

\iff

(iii)
and

(ii)

\iff

(iv)
were obtained in
Proposition
6.4
, and we obtain the equivalence

(iii)

\iff

(iv)
via Lemma

6.6
.
∎

Combining Corollaries
6.7
and
6.8
, we obtain

Theorem 6.9

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Theorem 2.5
)
.

The following are equivalent for a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
p\colon X\to Y
:

(i)

p
is a
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful lax epimorphism,

(ii)

\Cauchy p
is a
\mathcal{V}
-fully faithful lax epimorphism,

(iii)

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
is a fully faithful lax epimorphism,

(iv)

\Cauchy p
is an equivalence,

(v)

\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
is an equivalence.

Proof.

Corollaries
6.7
and
6.8
guarantee that

(i)

\iff

(ii)

\iff

(iii)
.

As any equivalence is a fully faithful lax epimorphism, we have

(iv)

\implies

(ii)
, and since
equivalences fix tiny objects, we conclude
(v)

\implies

(iv)
.

Finally, we note that we have an adjunction
\Lan_{p}\dashv\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathcal{V})
, so we obtain
(iii)

\implies

(v)
by Proposition
6.1
.
∎

6.3
Descent for the bifibration of split fibrations

Lemma 6.10

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Proposition 3.1
)
.

A functor
p\colon E\to B
between small categories is fully faithful
(respectively, a lax epimorphism) if and only if
\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathsf{Cat})
is a lax
epimorphism (respectively, fully faithful).

Proof.

We consider the fully faithful functor
J\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Cat}
, which
has left and right adjoint functors. Hence, its direct image
J_{!}\colon\mathsf{Set}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}\to\mathsf{Cat}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}
is a fully faithful 2-functor, and has
left and right 2-adjoints. By
Lucatelli & Sousa, 2022
, Remark 2.8 and Lemma 2.10
, we
conclude that
J_{!}
creates fully faithful morphisms and lax
epimorphisms.

Hence, we conclude from Corollary
6.8
(respectively, Corollary

6.7
) that
J_{!}p
is fully faithful (a lax epimorphism) if
and only if
\mathsf{Cat}\text{-}\mathsf{CAT}(J_{!}p,\mathsf{Cat})\cong\mathsf{CAT}(p,\mathsf{Cat})
is a lax
epimorphism (fully faithful).
∎

Theorem 6.11

(
Lucatelli et al., 2023
, Theorem 3.2
)
.

For a functor
p\colon E\to B
between small categories, we consider
the lax codescent factorization:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CoDesc}(\Ker(p))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(p)}}

The following are equivalent:

(i)

p
is an effective
F
-descent morphism (respectively,
F
-descent morphism),

(ii)

\mathsf{CAT}(\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(p)},\mathsf{Cat})
is an equivalence (respectively,
fully faithful),

(iii)

p
is an effective
F_{D}
-descent morphism (respectively,
F_{D}
-descent morphism),

(iv)

\mathsf{CAT}(\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(p)},\mathsf{Set})
is an equivalence (respectively,
fully faithful),

(v)

\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(p)}
is a fully faithful lax epimorphism
(respectively, a lax epimorphism),

(vi)

\Cauchy\mathcal{K}^{\Ker(p)}
is an equivalence (respectively, a
lax epimorphism),

Proof.

By Theorem
6.9
(respectively, Corollary
6.7
), we
deduce that
(v)

\iff

(vi)

\iff

(iv)
.

Since both
F
and
F_{D}
preserve lax descent objects, we conclude
by Lemma
2.13
that
(i)

\iff

(ii)
and
(iii)

\iff

(iv)
.

Finally, by Lemma
6.10
we obtain
(ii)

\iff

(v)
.
∎
